-->\u{A}<p>okay</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A processing instruction (type 3):\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<?php\u{A}\u{A}  echo '>';\u{A}\u{A}?>\u{A}okay\u{A}.\u{A}<?php\u{A}\u{A}  echo '>';\u{A}\u{A}?>\u{A}<p>okay</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A declaration (type 4):\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<!DOCTYPE html>\u{A}.\u{A}<!DOCTYPE html>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}CDATA (type 5):\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<![CDATA[\u{A}function matchwo(a,b)\u{A}{\u{A}  if (a < b && a < 0) then {\u{A}    return 1;\u{A}\u{A}  } else {\u{A}\u{A}    return 0;\u{A}  }\u{A}}\u{A}]]>\u{A}okay\u{A}.\u{A}<![CDATA[\u{A}function matchwo(a,b)\u{A}{\u{A}  if (a < b && a < 0) then {\u{A}    return 1;\u{A}\u{A}  } else {\u{A}\u{A}    return 0;\u{A}  }\u{A}}\u{A}]]>\u{A}<p>okay</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The opening tag can be preceded by up to three spaces of indentation, but not\u{A}four:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}  <!-- foo -->\u{A}\u{A}    <!-- foo -->\u{A}.\u{A}  <!-- foo -->\u{A}<pre><code>&lt;!-- foo --&gt;\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}  <div>\u{A}\u{A}    <div>\u{A}.\u{A}  <div>\u{A}<pre><code>&lt;div&gt;\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}An HTML block of types 1--6 can interrupt a paragraph, and need not be\u{A}preceded by a blank line.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}Foo\u{A}<div>\u{A}bar\u{A}</div>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>Foo</p>\u{A}<div>\u{A}bar\u{A}</div>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}However, a following blank line is needed, except at the end of\u{A}a document, and except for blocks of types 1--5, [above][HTML\u{A}block]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<div>\u{A}bar\u{A}</div>\u{A}*foo*\u{A}.\u{A}<div>\u{A}bar\u{A}</div>\u{A}*foo*\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}HTML blocks of type 7 cannot interrupt a paragraph:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}Foo\u{A}<a href=\u{22}bar\u{22}>\u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<p>Foo\u{A}<a href=\u{22}bar\u{22}>\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This rule differs from John Gruber's original Markdown syntax\u{A}specification, which says:\u{A}\u{A}> The only restrictions are that blo");
      out.append("ck-level HTML elements \u{2014}\u{A}> e.g. `<div>`, `<table>`, `<pre>`, `<p>`, etc. \u{2014} must be separated from\u{A}> surrounding content by blank lines, and the start and end tags of the\u{A}> block should not be indented with spaces or tabs.\u{A}\u{A}In some ways Gruber's rule is more restrictive than the one given\u{A}here:\u{A}\u{A}- It requires that an HTML block be preceded by a blank line.\u{A}- It does not allow the start tag to be indented.\u{A}- It requires a matching end tag, which it also does not allow to\u{A}  be indented.\u{A}\u{A}Most Markdown implementations (including some of Gruber's own) do not\u{A}respect all of these restrictions.\u{A}\u{A}There is one respect, however, in which Gruber's rule is more liberal\u{A}than the one given here, since it allows blank lines to occur inside\u{A}an HTML block.  There are two reasons for disallowing them here.\u{A}First, it removes the need to parse balanced tags, which is\u{A}expensive and can require backtracking from the end of the document\u{A}if no matching end tag is found. Second, it provides a very simple\u{A}and flexible way of including Markdown content inside HTML tags:\u{A}simply separate the Markdown from the HTML using blank lines:\u{A}\u{A}Compare:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<div>\u{A}\u{A}*Emphasized* text.\u{A}\u{A}</div>\u{A}.\u{A}<div>\u{A}<p><em>Emphasized</em> text.</p>\u{A}</div>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<div>\u{A}*Emphasized* text.\u{A}</div>\u{A}.\u{A}<div>\u{A}*Emphasized* text.\u{A}</div>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Some Markdown implementations have adopted a convention of\u{A}interpreting content inside tags as text if the open tag has\u{A}the attribute `markdown=1`.  The rule given above seems a simpler and\u{A}more elegant way of achieving the same expressive power, which is also\u{A}much simpler to parse.\u{A}\u{A}The main potential drawback is that one can no longer paste HTML\u{A}blocks into Markdown documents with 100% reliability.  However,\u{A}*in most cases* this will work fine, because the blank lines in\u{A}HTML are usually followed by HTML block tags.  For example:\u{A}\u{A}````````````````````````````");
      out.append("```` example\u{A}<table>\u{A}\u{A}<tr>\u{A}\u{A}<td>\u{A}Hi\u{A}</td>\u{A}\u{A}</tr>\u{A}\u{A}</table>\u{A}.\u{A}<table>\u{A}<tr>\u{A}<td>\u{A}Hi\u{A}</td>\u{A}</tr>\u{A}</table>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}There are problems, however, if the inner tags are indented\u{A}*and* separated by spaces, as then they will be interpreted as\u{A}an indented code block:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<table>\u{A}\u{A}  <tr>\u{A}\u{A}    <td>\u{A}      Hi\u{A}    </td>\u{A}\u{A}  </tr>\u{A}\u{A}</table>\u{A}.\u{A}<table>\u{A}  <tr>\u{A}<pre><code>&lt;td&gt;\u{A}  Hi\u{A}&lt;/td&gt;\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}  </tr>\u{A}</table>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Fortunately, blank lines are usually not necessary and can be\u{A}deleted.  The exception is inside `<pre>` tags, but as described\u{A}[above][HTML blocks], raw HTML blocks starting with `<pre>`\u{A}*can* contain blank lines.\u{A}\u{A}## Link reference definitions\u{A}\u{A}A [link reference definition](@)\u{A}consists of a [link label], optionally preceded by up to three spaces of\u{A}indentation, followed\u{A}by a colon (`:`), optional spaces or tabs (including up to one\u{A}[line ending]), a [link destination],\u{A}optional spaces or tabs (including up to one\u{A}[line ending]), and an optional [link\u{A}title], which if it is present must be separated\u{A}from the [link destination] by spaces or tabs.\u{A}No further character may occur.\u{A}\u{A}A [link reference definition]\u{A}does not correspond to a structural element of a document.  Instead, it\u{A}defines a label which can be used in [reference links]\u{A}and reference-style [images] elsewhere in the document.  [Link\u{A}reference definitions] can come either before or after the links that use\u{A}them.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}   [foo]: \u{A}      /url  \u{A}           'the title'  \u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}the title\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[Foo*bar\u{5C}]]:my_(url) 'title (with parens)'\u{A}\u{A}[Foo*bar\u{5C}]]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}my_(url)\u{22} title=\u{22}title (with parens)\u{22}>Foo*bar]</a></p>\u{A}``");
      out.append("``````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[Foo bar]:\u{A}<my url>\u{A}'title'\u{A}\u{A}[Foo bar]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}my%20url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>Foo bar</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The title may extend over multiple lines:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url '\u{A}title\u{A}line1\u{A}line2\u{A}'\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}\u{A}title\u{A}line1\u{A}line2\u{A}\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}However, it may not contain a [blank line]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url 'title\u{A}\u{A}with blank line'\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo]: /url 'title</p>\u{A}<p>with blank line'</p>\u{A}<p>[foo]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The title may be omitted:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]:\u{A}/url\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The link destination may not be omitted:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]:\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo]:</p>\u{A}<p>[foo]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A} However, an empty link destination may be specified using\u{A} angle brackets:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: <>\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}The title must be separated from the link destination by\u{A}spaces or tabs:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: <bar>(baz)\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo]: <bar>(baz)</p>\u{A}<p>[foo]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Both title and destination can contain backslash escapes\u{A}and literal backslashes:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url\u{5C}bar\u{5C}*baz \u{22}foo\u{5C}\u{22}bar\u{5C}baz\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url%5Cbar*baz\u{22} title=\u{22}foo&quot;bar\u{5C}baz\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A link can come before its corresponding definition:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]\u{A}\u{A}[foo]: url\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}url\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}If there are several matching definitions, the first one takes\u{A}precedence:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]\u{A}\u{A}[foo]: first\u{A}[foo]: second\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}first\u{22}>fo");
      out.append("o</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}As noted in the section on [Links], matching of labels is\u{A}case-insensitive (see [matches]).\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[FOO]: /url\u{A}\u{A}[Foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>Foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[\u{391}\u{393}\u{3A9}]: /\u{3C6}\u{3BF}\u{3C5}\u{A}\u{A}[\u{3B1}\u{3B3}\u{3C9}]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/%CF%86%CE%BF%CF%85\u{22}>\u{3B1}\u{3B3}\u{3C9}</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Whether something is a [link reference definition] is\u{A}independent of whether the link reference it defines is\u{A}used in the document.  Thus, for example, the following\u{A}document contains just a link reference definition, and\u{A}no visible content:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url\u{A}.\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Here is another one:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[\u{A}foo\u{A}]: /url\u{A}bar\u{A}.\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is not a link reference definition, because there are\u{A}characters other than spaces or tabs after the title:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url \u{22}title\u{22} ok\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo]: /url &quot;title&quot; ok</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is a link reference definition, but it has no title:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url\u{A}\u{22}title\u{22} ok\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&quot;title&quot; ok</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is not a link reference definition, because it is indented\u{A}four spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}    [foo]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<pre><code>[foo]: /url &quot;title&quot;\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<p>[foo]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is not a link reference definition, because it occurs inside\u{A}a code block:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}```\u{A}[foo]: /url\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<pre><code>[foo]: /url\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<p>[foo]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A [link reference definition] cannot interrupt a paragraph.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}Foo\u{A}[bar]: /baz\u{A}\u{A}[bar]\u{A}.\u{A}<p>Foo\u{A}[bar]: /baz</p>\u{A}<p>[bar]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}");
      out.append("\u{A}However, it can directly follow other block elements, such as headings\u{A}and thematic breaks, and it need not be followed by a blank line.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}# [Foo]\u{A}[foo]: /url\u{A}> bar\u{A}.\u{A}<h1><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>Foo</a></h1>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url\u{A}bar\u{A}===\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<h1>bar</h1>\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url\u{A}===\u{A}[foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p>===\u{A}<a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Several [link reference definitions]\u{A}can occur one after another, without intervening blank lines.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /foo-url \u{22}foo\u{22}\u{A}[bar]: /bar-url\u{A}  \u{22}bar\u{22}\u{A}[baz]: /baz-url\u{A}\u{A}[foo],\u{A}[bar],\u{A}[baz]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/foo-url\u{22} title=\u{22}foo\u{22}>foo</a>,\u{A}<a href=\u{22}/bar-url\u{22} title=\u{22}bar\u{22}>bar</a>,\u{A}<a href=\u{22}/baz-url\u{22}>baz</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}[Link reference definitions] can occur\u{A}inside block containers, like lists and block quotations.  They\u{A}affect the entire document, not just the container in which they\u{A}are defined:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]\u{A}\u{A}> [foo]: /url\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## Paragraphs\u{A}\u{A}A sequence of non-blank lines that cannot be interpreted as other\u{A}kinds of blocks forms a [paragraph](@).\u{A}The contents of the paragraph are the result of parsing the\u{A}paragraph's raw content as inlines.  The paragraph's raw content\u{A}is formed by concatenating the lines and removing initial and final\u{A}spaces or tabs.\u{A}\u{A}A simple example with two paragraphs:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}aaa\u{A}\u{A}bbb\u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa</p>\u{A}<p>bbb</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Paragraphs can contain multiple lines, but no blank lines:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}aaa\u{A}bbb\u{A}\u{A}ccc\u{A}ddd\u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa\u{A}bbb</p>\u{A}<p>ccc\u{A}ddd</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Multiple blank lines between pa");
      out.append("ragraphs have no effect:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}aaa\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}bbb\u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa</p>\u{A}<p>bbb</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Leading spaces or tabs are skipped:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}  aaa\u{A} bbb\u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa\u{A}bbb</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Lines after the first may be indented any amount, since indented\u{A}code blocks cannot interrupt paragraphs.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}aaa\u{A}             bbb\u{A}                                       ccc\u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa\u{A}bbb\u{A}ccc</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}However, the first line may be preceded by up to three spaces of indentation.\u{A}Four spaces of indentation is too many:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}   aaa\u{A}bbb\u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa\u{A}bbb</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}    aaa\u{A}bbb\u{A}.\u{A}<pre><code>aaa\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<p>bbb</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Final spaces or tabs are stripped before inline parsing, so a paragraph\u{A}that ends with two or more spaces will not end with a [hard line\u{A}break]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}aaa     \u{A}bbb     \u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa<br />\u{A}bbb</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## Blank lines\u{A}\u{A}[Blank lines] between block-level elements are ignored,\u{A}except for the role they play in determining whether a [list]\u{A}is [tight] or [loose].\u{A}\u{A}Blank lines at the beginning and end of the document are also ignored.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}  \u{A}\u{A}aaa\u{A}  \u{A}\u{A}# aaa\u{A}\u{A}  \u{A}.\u{A}<p>aaa</p>\u{A}<h1>aaa</h1>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}# Container blocks\u{A}\u{A}A [container block](#container-blocks) is a block that has other\u{A}blocks as its contents.  There are two basic kinds of container blocks:\u{A}[block quotes] and [list items].\u{A}[Lists] are meta-containers for [list items].\u{A}\u{A}We define the syntax for container blocks recursively.  The general\u{A}form of the definition is:\u{A}\u{A}> If X is a sequence of blocks, then the result of\u{A}> transforming X in such-and-such a way is a container of type Y\u{A}> with these blocks as its content.");
      out.append("\u{A}\u{A}So, we explain what counts as a block quote or list item by explaining\u{A}how these can be *generated* from their contents. This should suffice\u{A}to define the syntax, although it does not give a recipe for *parsing*\u{A}these constructions.  (A recipe is provided below in the section entitled\u{A}[A parsing strategy](#appendix-a-parsing-strategy).)\u{A}\u{A}## Block quotes\u{A}\u{A}A [block quote marker](@),\u{A}optionally preceded by up to three spaces of indentation,\u{A}consists of (a) the character `>` together with a following space of\u{A}indentation, or (b) a single character `>` not followed by a space of\u{A}indentation.\u{A}\u{A}The following rules define [block quotes]:\u{A}\u{A}1.  **Basic case.**  If a string of lines *Ls* constitute a sequence\u{A}    of blocks *Bs*, then the result of prepending a [block quote\u{A}    marker] to the beginning of each line in *Ls*\u{A}    is a [block quote](#block-quotes) containing *Bs*.\u{A}\u{A}2.  **Laziness.**  If a string of lines *Ls* constitute a [block\u{A}    quote](#block-quotes) with contents *Bs*, then the result of deleting\u{A}    the initial [block quote marker] from one or\u{A}    more lines in which the next character other than a space or tab after the\u{A}    [block quote marker] is [paragraph continuation\u{A}    text] is a block quote with *Bs* as its content.\u{A}    [Paragraph continuation text](@) is text\u{A}    that will be parsed as part of the content of a paragraph, but does\u{A}    not occur at the beginning of the paragraph.\u{A}\u{A}3.  **Consecutiveness.**  A document cannot contain two [block\u{A}    quotes] in a row unless there is a [blank line] between them.\u{A}\u{A}Nothing else counts as a [block quote](#block-quotes).\u{A}\u{A}Here is a simple example:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> # Foo\u{A}> bar\u{A}> baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<h1>Foo</h1>\u{A}<p>bar\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The space or tab after the `>` characters can be omitted:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}># Foo\u{A}>bar\u{A}> baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<h1>Foo</h1>\u{A}<p>bar\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The ");
      out.append("`>` characters can be preceded by up to three spaces of indentation:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}   > # Foo\u{A}   > bar\u{A} > baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<h1>Foo</h1>\u{A}<p>bar\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Four spaces of indentation is too many:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}    > # Foo\u{A}    > bar\u{A}    > baz\u{A}.\u{A}<pre><code>&gt; # Foo\u{A}&gt; bar\u{A}&gt; baz\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The Laziness clause allows us to omit the `>` before\u{A}[paragraph continuation text]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> # Foo\u{A}> bar\u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<h1>Foo</h1>\u{A}<p>bar\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A block quote can contain some lazy and some non-lazy\u{A}continuation lines:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> bar\u{A}baz\u{A}> foo\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bar\u{A}baz\u{A}foo</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Laziness only applies to lines that would have been continuations of\u{A}paragraphs had they been prepended with [block quote markers].\u{A}For example, the `> ` cannot be omitted in the second line of\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}> foo\u{A}> ---\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}without changing the meaning:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> foo\u{A}---\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<hr />\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Similarly, if we omit the `> ` in the second line of\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}> - foo\u{A}> - bar\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}then the block quote ends after the first line:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> - foo\u{A}- bar\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}For the same reason, we can't omit the `> ` in front of\u{A}subsequent lines of an indented or fenced code block:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}>     foo\u{A}    bar\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<pre><code>foo\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<pre><code>bar\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> ```\u{A}foo\u{A}```\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<pre><code></code></pre>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<pre><cod");
      out.append("e></code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that in the following case, we have a [lazy\u{A}continuation line]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> foo\u{A}    - bar\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo\u{A}- bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}To see why, note that in\u{A}\u{A}```markdown\u{A}> foo\u{A}>     - bar\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}the `- bar` is indented too far to start a list, and can't\u{A}be an indented code block because indented code blocks cannot\u{A}interrupt paragraphs, so it is [paragraph continuation text].\u{A}\u{A}A block quote can be empty:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}>\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}>\u{A}>  \u{A}> \u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A block quote can have initial or final blank lines:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}>\u{A}> foo\u{A}>  \u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A blank line always separates block quotes:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> foo\u{A}\u{A}> bar\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}(Most current Markdown implementations, including John Gruber's\u{A}original `Markdown.pl`, will parse this example as a single block quote\u{A}with two paragraphs.  But it seems better to allow the author to decide\u{A}whether two block quotes or one are wanted.)\u{A}\u{A}Consecutiveness means that if we put these block quotes together,\u{A}we get a single block quote:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> foo\u{A}> bar\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo\u{A}bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}To get a block quote with two paragraphs, use:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> foo\u{A}>\u{A}> bar\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Block quotes can interrupt paragraphs:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo\u{A}> bar\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}In g");
      out.append("eneral, blank lines are not needed before or after block\u{A}quotes:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> aaa\u{A}***\u{A}> bbb\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>aaa</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<hr />\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bbb</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}However, because of laziness, a blank line is needed between\u{A}a block quote and a following paragraph:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> bar\u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bar\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> bar\u{A}\u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<p>baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> bar\u{A}>\u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<p>baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}It is a consequence of the Laziness rule that any number\u{A}of initial `>`s may be omitted on a continuation line of a\u{A}nested block quote:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}> > > foo\u{A}bar\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo\u{A}bar</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}>>> foo\u{A}> bar\u{A}>>baz\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>foo\u{A}bar\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}When including an indented code block in a block quote,\u{A}remember that the [block quote marker] includes\u{A}both the `>` and a following space of indentation.  So *five spaces* are needed\u{A}after the `>`:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}>     code\u{A}\u{A}>    not code\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<pre><code>code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>not code</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## List items\u{A}\u{A}A [list marker](@) is a\u{A}[bullet list marker] or an [ordered list marker].\u{A}\u{A}A [bullet list marker](@)\u{A}is a `-`, `+`, or `*` character.\u{A}\u{A}An [ordered list marker](@)\u{A}is a sequence of 1--9 arabic digits (`0-9`), followed by either a\u{A}`.` character or a `)` character.  (The reason for the length\u{A}limit is that");
      out.append(" with 10 digits we start seeing integer overflows\u{A}in some browsers.)\u{A}\u{A}The following rules define [list items]:\u{A}\u{A}1.  **Basic case.**  If a sequence of lines *Ls* constitute a sequence of\u{A}    blocks *Bs* starting with a character other than a space or tab, and *M* is\u{A}    a list marker of width *W* followed by 1 \u{2264} *N* \u{2264} 4 spaces of indentation,\u{A}    then the result of prepending *M* and the following spaces to the first line\u{A}    of *Ls*, and indenting subsequent lines of *Ls* by *W + N* spaces, is a\u{A}    list item with *Bs* as its contents.  The type of the list item\u{A}    (bullet or ordered) is determined by the type of its list marker.\u{A}    If the list item is ordered, then it is also assigned a start\u{A}    number, based on the ordered list marker.\u{A}\u{A}    Exceptions:\u{A}\u{A}    1. When the first list item in a [list] interrupts\u{A}       a paragraph---that is, when it starts on a line that would\u{A}       otherwise count as [paragraph continuation text]---then (a)\u{A}       the lines *Ls* must not begin with a blank line, and (b) if\u{A}       the list item is ordered, the start number must be 1.\u{A}    2. If any line is a [thematic break][thematic breaks] then\u{A}       that line is not a list item.\u{A}\u{A}For example, let *Ls* be the lines\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}A paragraph\u{A}with two lines.\u{A}\u{A}    indented code\u{A}\u{A}> A block quote.\u{A}.\u{A}<p>A paragraph\u{A}with two lines.</p>\u{A}<pre><code>indented code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>A block quote.</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}And let *M* be the marker `1.`, and *N* = 2.  Then rule #1 says\u{A}that the following is an ordered list item with start number 1,\u{A}and the same contents as *Ls*:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1.  A paragraph\u{A}    with two lines.\u{A}\u{A}        indented code\u{A}\u{A}    > A block quote.\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>A paragraph\u{A}with two lines.</p>\u{A}<pre><code>indented code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>A block quote.</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The most important thing to notice is that the p");
      out.append("osition of\u{A}the text after the list marker determines how much indentation\u{A}is needed in subsequent blocks in the list item.  If the list\u{A}marker takes up two spaces of indentation, and there are three spaces between\u{A}the list marker and the next character other than a space or tab, then blocks\u{A}must be indented five spaces in order to fall under the list\u{A}item.\u{A}\u{A}Here are some examples showing how far content must be indented to be\u{A}put under the list item:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- one\u{A}\u{A} two\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>one</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}<p>two</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- one\u{A}\u{A}  two\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>one</p>\u{A}<p>two</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A} -    one\u{A}\u{A}     two\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>one</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}<pre><code> two\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A} -    one\u{A}\u{A}      two\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>one</p>\u{A}<p>two</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}It is tempting to think of this in terms of columns:  the continuation\u{A}blocks must be indented at least to the column of the first character other than\u{A}a space or tab after the list marker.  However, that is not quite right.\u{A}The spaces of indentation after the list marker determine how much relative\u{A}indentation is needed.  Which column this indentation reaches will depend on\u{A}how the list item is embedded in other constructions, as shown by\u{A}this example:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}   > > 1.  one\u{A}>>\u{A}>>     two\u{A}.\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>one</p>\u{A}<p>two</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Here `two` occurs in the same column as the list marker `1.`,\u{A}but is actually contained in the list item, because there is\u{A}sufficient indentation after the last containing blockquote marker.\u{A}\u{A}The converse is also possible.  In the following example, the word `two`\u{A}occurs far to the right of the initial text of the list item");
      out.append(", `one`, but\u{A}it is not considered part of the list item, because it is not indented\u{A}far enough past the blockquote marker:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` list item that contains an indented code block will preserve\u{A}empty lines within the code block verbatim.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- Foo\u{A}\u{A}      bar\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}      baz\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>Foo</p>\u{A}<pre><code>bar\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}baz\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}Note that start=\u{22}0\u{22}>\u{A}<li>ok</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}003. ok\u{A}.\u{A}<ol start=\u{22}3\u{22}>\u{A}<li>ok</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A start number may not be negative:\u{A}\u{A}``````````````````````````````");
      out.append("`` example\u{A}-1. not ok\u{A}.\u{A}<p>-1. not ok</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}2.  **Item starting with indented code.**  If a sequence of lines *Ls*\u{A}    constitute a sequence of blocks *Bs* starting with an indented code\u{A}    block, and *M* is a list marker of width *W* followed by\u{A}    one space of indentation, then the result of prepending *M* and the\u{A}    following space to the first line of *Ls*, and indenting subsequent lines\u{A}    of *Ls* by *W + 1* spaces, is a list item with *Bs* as its contents.\u{A}    If a line is empty, then it need not be indented.  The type of the\u{A}    list item (bullet or ordered) is determined by the type of its list\u{A}    marker.  If the list item is ordered, then it is also assigned a\u{A}    start number, based on the ordered list marker.\u{A}\u{A}An indented code block will have to be preceded by four spaces of indentation\u{A}beyond the edge of the region where text will be included in the list item.\u{A}In the following case that is 6 spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- foo\u{A}\u{A}      bar\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<pre><code>bar\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}And in this case it is 11 spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}  10.  foo\u{A}\u{A}           bar\u{A}.\u{A}<ol start=\u{22}10\u{22}>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<pre><code>bar\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}If the *first* block in the list item is an indented code block,\u{A}then by rule #2, the contents must be preceded by *one* space of indentation\u{A}after the list marker:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}    indented code\u{A}\u{A}paragraph\u{A}\u{A}    more code\u{A}.\u{A}<pre><code>indented code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<p>paragraph</p>\u{A}<pre><code>more code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1.     indented code\u{A}\u{A}   paragraph\u{A}\u{A}       more code\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<pre><code>indented code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<p>paragraph</p>\u{A}<pre><code>more code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that an additional space of indentati");
      out.append("on is interpreted as space\u{A}inside the code block:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1.      indented code\u{A}\u{A}   paragraph\u{A}\u{A}       more code\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<pre><code> indented code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<p>paragraph</p>\u{A}<pre><code>more code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that rules #1 and #2 only apply to two cases:  (a) cases\u{A}in which the lines to be included in a list item begin with a\u{A}character other than a space or tab, and (b) cases in which\u{A}they begin with an indented code\u{A}block. A sublist must be indented the same number\u{A}of spaces of indentation a paragraph would need to be in order to be included\u{A}in the list item.\u{A}\u{A}So, in this case we need two spaces indent:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- foo\u{A}  - bar\u{A}    - baz\u{A}      - boo\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>bar\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>baz\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>boo</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}One is not enough:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- foo\u{A} - bar\u{A}  - baz\u{A}   - boo\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}<li>baz</li>\u{A}<li>boo</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Here we need four, because the list marker is wider:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}10) foo\u{A}    - bar\u{A}.\u{A}<ol start=\u{22}10\u{22}>\u{A}<li>foo\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Three is not enough:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}10) foo\u{A}   - bar\u{A}.\u{A}<ol start=\u{22}10\u{22}>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>bar<");
      out.append("/li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A list may be the first block in a list item:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- - foo\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1. - 2. foo\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<ol start=\u{22}2\u{22}>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A list item can contain a heading:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- # Foo\u{A}- Bar\u{A}  ---\u{A}  baz\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<h1>Foo</h1>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<h2>Bar</h2>\u{A}baz</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}### Motivation\u{A}\u{A}John Gruber's Markdown spec says the following about list items:\u{A}\u{A}1. \u{22}List markers typically start at the left margin, but may be indented\u{A}   by up to three spaces. List markers must be followed by one or more\u{A}   spaces or a tab.\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}2. \u{22}To make lists look nice, you can wrap items with hanging indents....\u{A}   But if you don't want to, you don't have to.\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}3. \u{22}List items may consist of multiple paragraphs. Each subsequent\u{A}   paragraph in a list item must be indented by either 4 spaces or one\u{A}   tab.\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}4. \u{22}It looks nice if you indent every line of the subsequent paragraphs,\u{A}   but here again, Markdown will allow you to be lazy.\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}5. \u{22}To put a blockquote within a list item, the blockquote's `>`\u{A}   delimiters need to be indented.\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}6. \u{22}To put a code block within a list item, the code block needs to be\u{A}   indented twice \u{2014} 8 spaces or two tabs.\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}These rules specify that a paragraph under a list item must be indented\u{A}four spaces (presumably, from the left margin, rather than the start of\u{A}the list marker, but this is not said), and that code under a list item\u{A}must be indented eight spaces instead of the usual four.  They also say\u{A}that a block quote must be indented, but not by how much; however, the\u{A}example given has four spaces indentation.  Although nothing is said\u{A}about other kinds of block-level content, it is certainly reasonable to\u{A}infer that *all* block elements under a");
      out.append(" list item, including other\u{A}lists, must be indented four spaces.  This principle has been called the\u{A}*four-space rule*.\u{A}\u{A}The four-space rule is clear and principled, and if the reference\u{A}implementation `Markdown.pl` had followed it, it probably would have\u{A}become the standard.  However, `Markdown.pl` allowed paragraphs and\u{A}sublists to start with only two spaces indentation, at least on the\u{A}outer level.  Worse, its behavior was inconsistent: a sublist of an\u{A}outer-level list needed two spaces indentation, but a sublist of this\u{A}sublist needed three spaces.  It is not surprising, then, that different\u{A}implementations of Markdown have developed very different rules for\u{A}determining what comes under a list item.  (Pandoc and python-Markdown,\u{A}for example, stuck with Gruber's syntax description and the four-space\u{A}rule, while discount, redcarpet, marked, PHP Markdown, and others\u{A}followed `Markdown.pl`'s behavior more closely.)\u{A}\u{A}Unfortunately, given the divergences between implementations, there\u{A}is no way to give a spec for list items that will be guaranteed not\u{A}to break any existing documents.  However, the spec given here should\u{A}correctly handle lists formatted with either the four-space rule or\u{A}the more forgiving `Markdown.pl` behavior, provided they are laid out\u{A}in a way that is natural for a human to read.\u{A}\u{A}The strategy here is to let the width and indentation of the list marker\u{A}determine the indentation necessary for blocks to fall under the list\u{A}item, rather than having a fixed and arbitrary number.  The writer can\u{A}think of the body of the list item as a unit which gets indented to the\u{A}right enough to fit the list marker (and any indentation on the list\u{A}marker).  (The laziness rule, #5, then allows continuation lines to be\u{A}unindented if needed.)\u{A}\u{A}This rule is superior, we claim, to any rule requiring a fixed level of\u{A}indentation from the margin.  The four-space rule is clear but\u{A}unnatural. It is quite unintuitive that\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}- foo\u{A}\u{A}  bar\u{A}\u{A}  - baz\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}should be par");
      out.append("sed as two lists with an intervening paragraph,\u{A}\u{A}``` html\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>baz</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}as the four-space rule demands, rather than a single list,\u{A}\u{A}``` html\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>baz</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The choice of four spaces is arbitrary.  It can be learned, but it is\u{A}not likely to be guessed, and it trips up beginners regularly.\u{A}\u{A}Would it help to adopt a two-space rule?  The problem is that such\u{A}a rule, together with the rule allowing up to three spaces of indentation for\u{A}the initial list marker, allows text that is indented *less than* the\u{A}original list marker to be included in the list item. For example,\u{A}`Markdown.pl` parses\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}   - one\u{A}\u{A}  two\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}as a single list item, with `two` a continuation paragraph:\u{A}\u{A}``` html\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>one</p>\u{A}<p>two</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}and similarly\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}>   - one\u{A}>\u{A}>  two\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}as\u{A}\u{A}``` html\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>one</p>\u{A}<p>two</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}This is extremely unintuitive.\u{A}\u{A}Rather than requiring a fixed indent from the margin, we could require\u{A}a fixed indent (say, two spaces, or even one space) from the list marker (which\u{A}may itself be indented).  This proposal would remove the last anomaly\u{A}discussed.  Unlike the spec presented above, it would count the following\u{A}as a list item with a subparagraph, even though the paragraph `bar`\u{A}is not indented as far as the first paragraph `foo`:\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A} 10. foo\u{A}\u{A}   bar  \u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}Arguably this text does read like a list item with `bar` as a subparagraph,\u{A}which may count in favor of the proposal.  However, on this proposal indented\u{A}code would have to be indented six spaces after the list marker.  And this\u{A}would break a lot of existing Markdown, which has the pattern:\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}1.  foo\u{A}\u{A}        indented code\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}where the code is indented eight spaces.  The spec above, by contrast, will\u{A}parse this text as expected, since the code block's indentation is measured\u{A}from the beginning of `f");
      out.append("oo`.\u{A}\u{A}The one case that needs special treatment is a list item that *starts*\u{A}with indented code.  How much indentation is required in that case, since\u{A}we don't have a \u{22}first paragraph\u{22} to measure from?  Rule #2 simply stipulates\u{A}that in such cases, we require one space indentation from the list marker\u{A}(and then the normal four spaces for the indented code).  This will match the\u{A}four-space rule in cases where the list marker plus its initial indentation\u{A}takes four spaces (a common case), but diverge in other cases.\u{A}\u{A}## Lists\u{A}\u{A}A [list](@) is a sequence of one or more\u{A}list items [of the same type].  The list items\u{A}may be separated by any number of blank lines.\u{A}\u{A}Two list items are [of the same type](@)\u{A}if they begin with a [list marker] of the same type.\u{A}Two list markers are of the\u{A}same type if (a) they are bullet list markers using the same character\u{A}(`-`, `+`, or `*`) or (b) they are ordered list numbers with the same\u{A}delimiter (either `.` or `)`).\u{A}\u{A}A list is an [ordered list](@)\u{A}if its constituent list items begin with\u{A}[ordered list markers], and a\u{A}[bullet list](@) if its constituent list\u{A}items begin with [bullet list markers].\u{A}\u{A}The [start number](@)\u{A}of an [ordered list] is determined by the list number of\u{A}its initial list item.  The numbers of subsequent list items are\u{A}disregarded.\u{A}\u{A}A list is [loose](@) if any of its constituent\u{A}list items are separated by blank lines, or if any of its constituent\u{A}list items directly contain two block-level elements with a blank line\u{A}between them.  Otherwise a list is [tight](@).\u{A}(The difference in HTML output is that paragraphs in a loose list are\u{A}wrapped in `<p>` tags, while paragraphs in a tight list are not.)\u{A}\u{A}Changing the bullet or ordered list delimiter starts a new list:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- foo\u{A}- bar\u{A}+ baz\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>baz</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1. foo\u{A}2. bar\u{A}3) baz\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}");
      out.append("<ol start=\u{22}3\u{22}>\u{A}<li>baz</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}In CommonMark, a list can interrupt a paragraph. That is,\u{A}no blank line is needed to separate a paragraph from a following\u{A}list:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}Foo\u{A}- bar\u{A}- baz\u{A}.\u{A}<p>Foo</p>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}<li>baz</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}`Markdown.pl` does not allow this, through fear of triggering a list\u{A}via a numeral in a hard-wrapped line:\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}The number of windows in my house is\u{A}14.  The number of doors is 6.\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}Oddly, though, `Markdown.pl` *does* allow a blockquote to\u{A}interrupt a paragraph, even though the same considerations might\u{A}apply.\u{A}\u{A}In CommonMark, we do allow lists to interrupt paragraphs, for\u{A}two reasons.  First, it is natural and not uncommon for people\u{A}to start lists without blank lines:\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}I need to buy\u{A}- new shoes\u{A}- a coat\u{A}- a plane ticket\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}Second, we are attracted to a\u{A}\u{A}> [principle of uniformity](@):\u{A}> if a chunk of text has a certain\u{A}> meaning, it will continue to have the same meaning when put into a\u{A}> container block (such as a list item or blockquote).\u{A}\u{A}(Indeed, the spec for [list items] and [block quotes] presupposes\u{A}this principle.) This principle implies that if\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}  * I need to buy\u{A}    - new shoes\u{A}    - a coat\u{A}    - a plane ticket\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}is a list item containing a paragraph followed by a nested sublist,\u{A}as all Markdown implementations agree it is (though the paragraph\u{A}may be rendered without `<p>` tags, since the list is \u{22}tight\u{22}),\u{A}then\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}I need to buy\u{A}- new shoes\u{A}- a coat\u{A}- a plane ticket\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}by itself should be a paragraph followed by a nested sublist.\u{A}\u{A}Since it is well established Markdown practice to allow lists to\u{A}interrupt paragraphs inside list items, the [principle of\u{A}uniformity] requires us to allow this outside list items as\u{A}well.  ([reStructuredText](https://docutils.sourceforge.net/rst.html)\u{A}takes a different approach, requiring blank lines before lists\u{A}even inside other list ite");
      out.append("ms.)\u{A}\u{A}In order to solve the problem of unwanted lists in paragraphs with\u{A}hard-wrapped numerals, we allow only lists starting with `1` to\u{A}interrupt paragraphs.  Thus,\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}The number of windows in my house is\u{A}14.  The number of doors is 6.\u{A}.\u{A}<p>The number of windows in my house is\u{A}14.  The number of doors is 6.</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}We may still get an unintended result in cases like\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}The number of windows in my house is\u{A}1.  The number of doors is 6.\u{A}.\u{A}<p>The number of windows in my house is</p>\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>The number of doors is 6.</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}but this rule should prevent most spurious list captures.\u{A}\u{A}There can be any number of blank lines between items:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- foo\u{A}\u{A}- bar\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}- baz\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>baz</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- foo\u{A}  - bar\u{A}    - baz\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}      bim\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>bar\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>baz</p>\u{A}<p>bim</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}To separate consecutive lists of the same type, or to separate a\u{A}list from an indented code block that would otherwise be parsed\u{A}as a subparagraph of the final list item, you can insert a blank HTML\u{A}comment:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- foo\u{A}- bar\u{A}\u{A}<!-- -->\u{A}\u{A}- baz\u{A}- bim\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>foo</li>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}<!-- -->\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>baz</li>\u{A}<li>bim</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}-   foo\u{A}\u{A}    notcode\u{A}\u{A}-   foo\u{A}\u{A}<!-- -->\u{A}\u{A}    code\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<p>notcode</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}<!-- -->\u{A}<pre><code>code\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}List items need not be indented to the same level.  The following\u{A}list items will be treated as items at the same list level,\u{A}since none is indented enough to belong to the previous list\u{A}");
      out.append("item:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A} - b\u{A}  - c\u{A}   - d\u{A}  - e\u{A} - f\u{A}- g\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a</li>\u{A}<li>b</li>\u{A}<li>c</li>\u{A}<li>d</li>\u{A}<li>e</li>\u{A}<li>f</li>\u{A}<li>g</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1. a\u{A}\u{A}  2. b\u{A}\u{A}   3. c\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>a</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>c</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}Note, however, that list items may not be preceded by more than\u{A}three spaces of indentation.  Here `- e` is treated as a paragraph continuation\u{A}line, because it is indented more than three spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A} - b\u{A}  - c\u{A}   - d\u{A}    - e\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a</li>\u{A}<li>b</li>\u{A}<li>c</li>\u{A}<li>d\u{A}- e</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}And here, `3. c` is treated as in indented code block,\u{A}because it is indented four spaces and preceded by a\u{A}blank line.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1. a\u{A}\u{A}  2. b\u{A}\u{A}    3. c\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>a</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}<pre><code>3. c\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is a loose list, because there is a blank line between\u{A}two of the list items:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}- b\u{A}\u{A}- c\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>a</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>c</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}So is this, with a empty second item:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}* a\u{A}*\u{A}\u{A}* c\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>a</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li></li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>c</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}These are loose lists, even though there are no blank lines between the items,\u{A}because one of the items directly contains two block-level elements\u{A}with a blank line between them:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}- b\u{A}\u{A}  c\u{A}- d\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>a</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}<p>c</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>d</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}- b\u{A}\u{A}  [ref]: /url\u{A}- d\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>a</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>d</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}``````````````````````");
      out.append("``````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is a tight list, because the blank lines are in a code block:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}- ```\u{A}  b\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}  ```\u{A}- c\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a</li>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<pre><code>b\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>c</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is a tight list, because the blank line is between two\u{A}paragraphs of a sublist.  So the sublist is loose while\u{A}the outer list is tight:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}  - b\u{A}\u{A}    c\u{A}- d\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}<p>c</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>d</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is a tight list, because the blank line is inside the\u{A}block quote:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}* a\u{A}  > b\u{A}  >\u{A}* c\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>c</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This list is tight, because the consecutive block elements\u{A}are not separated by blank lines:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}  > b\u{A}  ```\u{A}  c\u{A}  ```\u{A}- d\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a\u{A}<blockquote>\u{A}<p>b</p>\u{A}</blockquote>\u{A}<pre><code>c\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}</li>\u{A}<li>d</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A single-paragraph list is tight:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}  - b\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>a\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>b</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This list is loose, because of the blank line between the\u{A}two block elements in the list item:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}1. ```\u{A}   foo\u{A}   ```\u{A}\u{A}   bar\u{A}.\u{A}<ol>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<pre><code>foo\u{A}</code></pre>\u{A}<p>bar</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ol>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Here the outer list is loose, the inner list tight:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}* foo\u{A}  * bar\u{A}\u{A}  baz\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>bar</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}<p>baz</p>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}- a\u{A}  - b\u{A}  - c\u{A}\u{A}- d\u{A}  - e\u{A}  - f\u{A}.\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>a</p>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>b</li>\u{A}<li>c</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li");
      out.append(">\u{A}<li>\u{A}<p>d</p>\u{A}<ul>\u{A}<li>e</li>\u{A}<li>f</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}</li>\u{A}</ul>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}# Inlines\u{A}\u{A}Inlines are parsed sequentially from the beginning of the character\u{A}stream to the end (left to right, in left-to-right languages).\u{A}Thus, for example, in\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`hi`lo`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>hi</code>lo`</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}`hi` is parsed as code, leaving the backtick at the end as a literal\u{A}backtick.\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## Code spans\u{A}\u{A}A [backtick string](@)\u{A}is a string of one or more backtick characters (`` ` ``) that is neither\u{A}preceded nor followed by a backtick.\u{A}\u{A}A [code span](@) begins with a backtick string and ends with\u{A}a backtick string of equal length.  The contents of the code span are\u{A}the characters between these two backtick strings, normalized in the\u{A}following ways:\u{A}\u{A}- First, [line endings] are converted to [spaces].\u{A}- If the resulting string both begins *and* ends with a [space]\u{A}  character, but does not consist entirely of [space]\u{A}  characters, a single [space] character is removed from the\u{A}  front and back.  This allows you to include code that begins\u{A}  or ends with backtick characters, which must be separated by\u{A}  whitespace from the opening or closing backtick strings.\u{A}\u{A}This is a simple code span:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`foo`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Here two backticks are used, because the code contains a backtick.\u{A}This example also illustrates stripping of a single leading and\u{A}trailing space:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`` foo ` bar ``\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo ` bar</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This example shows the motivation for stripping leading and trailing\u{A}spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}` `` `\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>``</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}Note that only *one* space is stripped:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`  ``  `\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code> `` </code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}The stripping only");
      out.append(" happens if the space is on both\u{A}sides of the string:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}` a`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code> a</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}Only [spaces], and not [unicode whitespace] in general, are\u{A}stripped in this way:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`\u{A0}b\u{A0}`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>\u{A0}b\u{A0}</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}No stripping occurs if the code span contains only spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`\u{A0}`\u{A}`  `\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>\u{A0}</code>\u{A}<code>  </code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}[Line endings] are treated like spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}``\u{A}foo\u{A}bar  \u{A}baz\u{A}``\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo bar   baz</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}``\u{A}foo \u{A}``\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo </code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Interior spaces are not collapsed:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`foo   bar \u{A}baz`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo   bar  baz</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}Note that browsers will typically collapse consecutive spaces\u{A}when rendering `<code>` elements, so it is recommended that\u{A}the following CSS be used:\u{A}\u{A}    code{white-space: pre-wrap;}\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that backslash escapes do not work in code spans. All backslashes\u{A}are treated literally:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`foo\u{5C}`bar`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo\u{5C}</code>bar`</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Backslash escapes are never needed, because one can always choose a\u{A}string of *n* backtick characters as delimiters, where the code does\u{A}not contain any strings of exactly *n* backtick characters.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}``foo`bar``\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo`bar</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}` foo `` bar `\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>foo `` bar</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Code span backticks have higher precedence than any other inline\u{A}constructs except HTML tags and autolinks.  Thus, for example, this is\u{A}not parsed as emphasized text, since the ");
      out.append("second `*` is part of a code\u{A}span:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}*foo`*`\u{A}.\u{A}<p>*foo<code>*</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}And this is not parsed as a link:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[not a `link](/foo`)\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[not a <code>link](/foo</code>)</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Code spans, HTML tags, and autolinks have the same precedence.\u{A}Thus, this is code:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`<a href=\u{22}`\u{22}>`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>&lt;a href=&quot;</code>&quot;&gt;`</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}But this is an HTML tag:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a href=\u{22}`\u{22}>`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}`\u{22}>`</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}And this is code:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`<https://foo.bar.`baz>`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>&lt;https://foo.bar.</code>baz&gt;`</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}But this is an autolink:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<https://foo.bar.`baz>`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}https://foo.bar.%60baz\u{22}>https://foo.bar.`baz</a>`</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}When a backtick string is not closed by a matching backtick string,\u{A}we just have literal backticks:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}```foo``\u{A}.\u{A}<p>```foo``</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`foo\u{A}.\u{A}<p>`foo</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}The following case also illustrates the need for opening and\u{A}closing backtick strings to be equal in length:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`foo``bar``\u{A}.\u{A}<p>`foo<code>bar</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## Emphasis and strong emphasis\u{A}\u{A}John Gruber's original [Markdown syntax\u{A}description](https://daringfireball.net/projects/markdown/syntax#em) says:\u{A}\u{A}> Markdown treats asterisks (`*`) and underscores (`_`) as indicators of\u{A}> emphasis. Text wrapped with one `*` or `_` will be wrapped with an HTML\u{A}> `<em>` tag; double `*`'s or `_`'s will be wrapped with an HTML `<strong>`\u{A}> tag.\u{A}\u{A}This is enough for most users, but these ru");
      out.append("les leave much undecided,\u{A}especially when it comes to nested emphasis.  The original\u{A}`Markdown.pl` test suite makes it clear that triple `***` and\u{A}`___` delimiters can be used for strong emphasis, and most\u{A}implementations have also allowed the following patterns:\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}***strong emph***\u{A}***strong** in emph*\u{A}***emph* in strong**\u{A}**in strong *emph***\u{A}*in emph **strong***\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The following patterns are less widely supported, but the intent\u{A}is clear and they are useful (especially in contexts like bibliography\u{A}entries):\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}*emph *with emph* in it*\u{A}**strong **with strong** in it**\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}Many implementations have also restricted intraword emphasis to\u{A}the `*` forms, to avoid unwanted emphasis in words containing\u{A}internal underscores.  (It is best practice to put these in code\u{A}spans, but users often do not.)\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}internal emphasis: foo*bar*baz\u{A}no emphasis: foo_bar_baz\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The rules given below capture all of these patterns, while allowing\u{A}for efficient parsing strategies that do not backtrack.\u{A}\u{A}First, some definitions.  A [delimiter run](@) is either\u{A}a sequence of one or more `*` characters that is not preceded or\u{A}followed by a non-backslash-escaped `*` character, or a sequence\u{A}of one or more `_` characters that is not preceded or followed by\u{A}a non-backslash-escaped `_` character.\u{A}\u{A}A [left-flanking delimiter run](@) is\u{A}a [delimiter run] that is (1) not followed by [Unicode whitespace],\u{A}and either (2a) not followed by a [Unicode punctuation character], or\u{A}(2b) followed by a [Unicode punctuation character] and\u{A}preceded by [Unicode whitespace] or a [Unicode punctuation character].\u{A}For purposes of this definition, the beginning and the end of\u{A}the line count as Unicode whitespace.\u{A}\u{A}A [right-flanking delimiter run](@) is\u{A}a [delimiter run] that is (1) not preceded by [Unicode whitespace],\u{A}and either (2a) not preceded by a [Unicode punctuation character], or\u{A}(2b) preceded by a [Unicode punctuation character] and\u{A}followed by [Unicode whitespace] or a [Un");
      out.append("icode punctuation character].\u{A}For purposes of this definition, the beginning and the end of\u{A}the line count as Unicode whitespace.\u{A}\u{A}Here are some examples of delimiter runs.\u{A}\u{A}  - left-flanking but not right-flanking:\u{A}\u{A}    ```\u{A}    ***abc\u{A}      _abc\u{A}    **\u{22}abc\u{22}\u{A}     _\u{22}abc\u{22}\u{A}    ```\u{A}\u{A}  - right-flanking but not left-flanking:\u{A}\u{A}    ```\u{A}     abc***\u{A}     abc_\u{A}    \u{22}abc\u{22}**\u{A}    \u{22}abc\u{22}_\u{A}    ```\u{A}\u{A}  - Both left and right-flanking:\u{A}\u{A}    ```\u{A}     abc***def\u{A}    \u{22}abc\u{22}_\u{22}def\u{22}\u{A}    ```\u{A}\u{A}  - Neither left nor right-flanking:\u{A}\u{A}    ```\u{A}    abc *** def\u{A}    a _ b\u{A}    ```\u{A}\u{A}(The idea of distinguishing left-flanking and right-flanking\u{A}delimiter runs based on the character before and the character\u{A}after comes from Roopesh Chander's\u{A}[vfmd](https://web.archive.org/web/20220608143320/http://www.vfmd.org/vfmd-spec/specification/#procedure-for-identifying-emphasis-tags).\u{A}vfmd uses the terminology \u{22}emphasis indicator string\u{22} instead of \u{22}delimiter\u{A}run,\u{22} and its rules for distinguishing left- and right-flanking runs\u{A}are a bit more complex than the ones given here.)\u{A}\u{A}The following rules define emphasis and strong emphasis:\u{A}\u{A}1.  A single `*` character [can open emphasis](@)\u{A}    iff (if and only if) it is part of a [left-flanking delimiter run].\u{A}\u{A}2.  A single `_` character [can open emphasis] iff\u{A}    it is part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    and either (a) not part of a [right-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    or (b) part of a [right-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    preceded by a [Unicode punctuation character].\u{A}\u{A}3.  A single `*` character [can close emphasis](@)\u{A}    iff it is part of a [right-flanking delimiter run].\u{A}\u{A}4.  A single `_` character [can close emphasis] iff\u{A}    it is part of a [right-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    and either (a) not part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    or (b) part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    followed by a [Unicode punctuation character].\u{A}\u{A}5.  A double `**` [can open strong emphasis](@)\u{A}    iff it is part of a [left-flanking delimiter run].\u{A}\u{A}6.  A double `__` [can open ");
      out.append("strong emphasis] iff\u{A}    it is part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    and either (a) not part of a [right-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    or (b) part of a [right-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    preceded by a [Unicode punctuation character].\u{A}\u{A}7.  A double `**` [can close strong emphasis](@)\u{A}    iff it is part of a [right-flanking delimiter run].\u{A}\u{A}8.  A double `__` [can close strong emphasis] iff\u{A}    it is part of a [right-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    and either (a) not part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    or (b) part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]\u{A}    followed by a [Unicode punctuation character].\u{A}\u{A}9.  Emphasis begins with a delimiter that [can open emphasis] and ends\u{A}    with a delimiter that [can close emphasis], and that uses the same\u{A}    character (`_` or `*`) as the opening delimiter.  The\u{A}    opening and closing delimiters must belong to separate\u{A}    [delimiter runs].  If one of the delimiters can both\u{A}    open and close emphasis, then the sum of the lengths of the\u{A}    delimiter runs containing the opening and closing delimiters\u{A}    must not be a multiple of 3 unless both lengths are\u{A}    multiples of 3.\u{A}\u{A}10. Strong emphasis begins with a delimiter that\u{A}    [can open strong emphasis] and ends with a delimiter that\u{A}    [can close strong emphasis], and that uses the same character\u{A}    (`_` or `*`) as the opening delimiter.  The\u{A}    opening and closing delimiters must belong to separate\u{A}    [delimiter runs].  If one of the delimiters can both open\u{A}    and close strong emphasis, then the sum of the lengths of\u{A}    the delimiter runs containing the opening and closing\u{A}    delimiters must not be a multiple of 3 unless both lengths\u{A}    are multiples of 3.\u{A}\u{A}11. A literal `*` character cannot occur at the beginning or end of\u{A}    `*`-delimited emphasis or `**`-delimited strong emphasis, unless it\u{A}    is backslash-escaped.\u{A}\u{A}12. A literal `_` character cannot occur at the beginning or end of\u{A}    `_`-delimited emphasis or `__`-delimited strong emphasis, unless it\u{A}    i");
      out.append("s backslash-escaped.\u{A}\u{A}Where rules 1--12 above are compatible with multiple parsings,\u{A}the following principles resolve ambiguity:\u{A}\u{A}13. The number of nestings should be minimized. Thus, for example,\u{A}    an interpretation `<strong>...</strong>` is always preferred to\u{A}    `<em><em>...</em></em>`.\u{A}\u{A}14. An interpretation `<em><strong>...</strong></em>` is always\u{A}    preferred to `<strong><em>...</em></strong>`.\u{A}\u{A}15. When two potential emphasis or strong emphasis spans overlap,\u{A}    so that the second begins before the first ends and ends after\u{A}    the first ends, the first takes precedence. Thus, for example,\u{A}    `*foo _bar* baz_` is parsed as `<em>foo _bar</em> baz_` rather\u{A}    than `*foo <em>bar* baz</em>`.\u{A}\u{A}16. When there are two potential emphasis or strong emphasis spans\u{A}    with the same closing delimiter, the shorter one (the one that\u{A}    opens later) takes precedence. Thus, for example,\u{A}    `**foo **bar baz**` is parsed as `**foo <strong>bar baz</strong>`\u{A}    rather than `<strong>foo **bar baz</strong>`.\u{A}\u{A}17. Inline code spans, links, images, and HTML tags group more tightly\u{A}    than emphasis.  So, when there is a choice between an interpretation\u{A}    that contains one of these elements and one that does not, the\u{A}    former always wins.  Thus, for example, `*[foo*](bar)` is\u{A}    parsed as `*<a href=\u{22}bar\u{22}>foo*</a>` rather than as\u{A}    `<em>[foo</em>](bar)`.\u{A}\u{A}These rules can be illustrated through a series of examples.\u{A}\u{A}Rule 1:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}*foo bar*\u{A}.\u{A}<p><em>foo bar</em></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is not emphasis, because the opening `*` is followed by\u{A}whitespace, and hence not part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}a * foo bar*\u{A}.\u{A}<p>a * foo bar*</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is not emphasis, because the opening `*` is preceded\u{A}by an alphanumeric and followed by punctuation, and hence\u{A}not part of a [left-flanking delimiter run]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` exam");
      out.append("ple\u{A}a*\u{22}foo\u{22}*\u{A}.\u{A}<p>a*&quot;foo&quot;*</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Unicode nonbreaking spaces count as whitespace, too:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}*\u{A0}a\u{A0}*\u{A}.\u{A}<p>*\u{A0}a\u{A0}*</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Unicode symbols count as punctuation, too:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}*\u{24}*alpha.\u{A}\u{A}*\u{A3}*bravo.\u{A}\u{A}*\u{20AC}*charlie.\u{A}.\u{A}<p>*\u{24}*alpha.</p>\u{A}<p>*\u{A3}*bravo.</p>\u{A}<p>*\u{20AC}*charlie.</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Intraword emphasis with `*` is permitted:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo*bar*\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo<em>bar</em></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}5*6*78\u{A}.\u{A}<p>5<em>6</em>78</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Rule 2:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}_foo bar_\u{A}.\u{A}<p><em>foo bar</em></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is not emphasis, because the opening `_` is followed by\u{A}whitespace:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}_ foo bar_\u{A}.\u{A}<p>_ foo bar_</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is not emphasis, because the opening `_` is preceded\u{A}by an alphanumeric and followed by punctuation:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}a_\u{22}foo\u{22}_\u{A}.\u{A}<p>a_&quot;foo&quot;_</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Emphasis with `_` is not example\u{A}\u{43F}\u{440}\u{438}\u{441}\u{442}\u{430}\u{43D}\u{44F}\u{43C}_\u{441}\u{442}\u{440}\u{435}\u{43C}\u{44F}\u{442}\u{441}\u{44F}_\u{A}.\u{A}<p>\u{43F}\u{440}\u{438}\u{441}\u{442}\u{430}\u{43D}\u{44F}\u{43C}_\u{441}\u{442}\u{440}\u{435}\u{43C}\u{44F}\u{442}\u{441}\u{44F}_</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Here right-flanking, because it is preceded by\u{A}punctuation:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo-");
      out.append("_(bar)_\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo-<em>(bar)</em></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Rule 3:\u{A}\u{A}This is not emphasis, because the closing delimiter does\u{A}not match
