In [inline links] the\u{A}destination and title are given immediately after the link text.  In\u{A}[reference links] the destination and title are defined elsewhere in\u{A}the document.\u{A}\u{A}A [link text](@) consists of a sequence of zero or more\u{A}inline elements enclosed by square brackets (`[` and `]`).  The\u{A}following rules apply:\u{A}\u{A}- Links may not contain other links, at any level of nesting. If\u{A}  multiple otherwise valid link definitions appear nested inside each\u{A}  other, the inne");
      out.append("r-most definition is used.\u{A}\u{A}- Brackets are allowed in the [link text] only if (a) they\u{A}  are backslash-escaped or (b) they appear as a matched pair of brackets,\u{A}  with an open bracket `[`, a sequence of zero or more inlines, and\u{A}  a close bracket `]`.\u{A}\u{A}- Backtick [code spans], [autolinks], and raw [HTML tags] bind more tightly\u{A}  than the brackets in link text.  Thus, for example,\u{A}  `` [foo`]` `` could not be a link text, since the second `]`\u{A}  is part of a code span.\u{A}\u{A}- The brackets in link text bind more tightly than markers for\u{A}  [emphasis and strong emphasis]. Thus, for example, `*[foo*](url)` is a link.\u{A}\u{A}A [link destination](@) consists of either\u{A}\u{A}- a sequence of zero or more characters between an opening `<` and a\u{A}  closing `>` that contains no line endings or unescaped\u{A}  `<` or `>` characters, or\u{A}\u{A}- a nonempty sequence of characters that does not start with `<`,\u{A}  does not include [ASCII control characters][ASCII control character]\u{A}  or [space] character, and includes parentheses only if (a) they are\u{A}  backslash-escaped or (b) they are part of a balanced pair of\u{A}  unescaped parentheses.\u{A}  (Implementations may impose limits on parentheses nesting to\u{A}  avoid performance issues, but at least three levels of nesting\u{A}  should be supported.)\u{A}\u{A}A [link title](@)  consists of either\u{A}\u{A}- a sequence of zero or more characters between straight double-quote\u{A}  characters (`\u{22}`), including a `\u{22}` character only if it is\u{A}  backslash-escaped, or\u{A}\u{A}- a sequence of zero or more characters between straight single-quote\u{A}  characters (`'`), including a `'` character only if it is\u{A}  backslash-escaped, or\u{A}\u{A}- a sequence of zero or more characters between matching parentheses\u{A}  (`(...)`), including a `(` or `)` character only if it is\u{A}  backslash-escaped.\u{A}\u{A}Although [link titles] may span multiple lines, they may not contain\u{A}a [blank line].\u{A}\u{A}An [inline link](@) consists of a [link text] followed immediately\u{A}by a left parenthesis `(`, an optional [link destination], an optional\u{A}[link title],");
      out.append(" and a right parenthesis `)`.\u{A}These four components may be separated by spaces, tabs, and up to one line\u{A}ending.\u{A}If both [link destination] and [link title] are present, they *must* be\u{A}separated by spaces, tabs, and up to one line ending.\u{A}\u{A}The link's text consists of the inlines contained\u{A}in the [link text] (excluding the enclosing square brackets).\u{A}The link's URI consists of the link destination, excluding enclosing\u{A}`<...>` if present, with backslash-escapes in effect as described\u{A}above.  The link's title consists of the link title, excluding its\u{A}enclosing delimiters, with backslash-escapes in effect as described\u{A}above.\u{A}\u{A}Here is a simple inline link:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/uri \u{22}title\u{22})\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/uri\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The title, the link text and even \u{A}the destination may be omitted:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/uri)\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/uri\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[](./target.md)\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}./target.md\u{22}></a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link]()\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](<>)\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[]()\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}\u{22}></a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}The destination can only contain spaces if it is\u{A}enclosed in pointy brackets:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/my uri)\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[link](/my uri)</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](</my uri>)\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/my%20uri\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}The destination cannot contain line endings,\u{A}even if enclosed in pointy brackets:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](foo\u{A}bar)\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[link](foo\u{A}bar)</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}``````````````````");

example\u{A}[a](<b)c\u{A}[a](<b)c>\u{A}[a](<b>c)\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[a](&lt;b)c\u{A}[a](&lt;b)c&gt;\u{A}[a](<b>c)</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}Parentheses inside the link destination may be escaped:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](\u{5C}(foo\u{5C}))\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}(foo)\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}Any number of parentheses are allowed without escaping, as long as they are\u{A}balanced:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](foo(and(bar)))\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}foo(and(bar))\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}However, if you have unbalanced parentheses, you need to escape or use the\u{A}`<...>` example\u{A}[link](foo\u{5C}(and\u{5C}(bar\u{5C}))\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}foo(and(bar)\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](<foo(and(bar)>)\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}foo(and(bar)\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Parentheses and other symbols can also be escaped, as Renderers may make different decisions\u{A}about how to escape or normalize URLs in the output.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](foo%20b&auml;)\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}foo%20b%C3%A4\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that, because titles can often be parsed as destinations,\u{A}if you try to omit the destination and keep the title, you'll\u{A}get unexpected results:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](\u{22}title\u{22})\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}%22title%22\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Titles may be in single quotes, double quotes, or parentheses:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/url \u{22}title\u{22})\u{A}[link](/url 'title')\u{A}[link](/url (title))\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>link</a>\u{A}<a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>link</a>\u{A}<a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Backslash escapes and entity and numeric character references\u{A}may be used in titles:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/url \u{22}title \u{5C}\u{22}&quot;\u{22})\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title &quot;&quot;\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Titles must be separated from the link using spaces, tabs, and up to one line\u{A}ending.\u{A}Other [Unicode whitespace] lik");
      out.append("e non-breaking space doesn't work.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/url\u{A0}\u{22}title\u{22})\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url%C2%A0%22title%22\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Nested balanced quotes are not allowed without escaping:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/url \u{22}title \u{22}and\u{22} title\u{22})\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[link](/url &quot;title &quot;and&quot; title&quot;)</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}But it is easy to work around this by using a different quote type:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](/url 'title \u{22}and\u{22} title')\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title &quot;and&quot; title\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}(Note:  `Markdown.pl` did allow double quotes inside a double-quoted\u{A}title, and its test suite included a test demonstrating this.\u{A}But it is hard to see a good rationale for the extra complexity this\u{A}brings, since there are already many ways---backslash escaping,\u{A}entity and numeric character references, or using a different\u{A}quote type for the enclosing title---to write titles containing\u{A}double quotes.  `Markdown.pl`'s handling of titles has a number\u{A}of other strange features. And, in\u{A}reference links but not inline links, it allows a title to begin\u{A}with `\u{22}` and end with `)`.  `Markdown.pl` 1.0.1 even allows\u{A}titles with no closing quotation mark, though 1.0.2b8 does not.\u{A}It seems preferable to adopt a simple, rational rule that works\u{A}the same way in inline links and link reference definitions.)\u{A}\u{A}Spaces, tabs, and up to one line ending is allowed around the destination and\u{A}title:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link](   /uri\u{A}  \u{22}title\u{22}  )\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/uri\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>link</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}But it is not allowed between the link text and the\u{A}following parenthesis:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[link] (/uri)\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[link] (/uri)</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The link text may cont");

A link\u{A}label can have at most 999 characters inside the square\u{A}brackets.\u{A}\u{A}One label [matches](@)\u{A}another just in case their normalized forms are equal.  To normalize a\u{A}label, strip off the opening and closing brackets,\u{A}perform the *Unicode case fold*, strip leading and trailing\u{A}spaces, tabs, and line endings, and collapse consecutive internal\u{A}spaces, tabs, and line endings to a single space.  If there are multiple\u{A}matching reference link definitions, the one that comes first in the\u{A}document is used.  (It is desirable in such cases to emit a warning.)\u{A}\u{A}The link's URI and title are provided by the matching [link\u{A}reference definition].\u{A}\u{A}Here is a simple example:\u{A}\u{A}````");

title=\u{22}title\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Unicode case fold is used:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[\u{1E9E}]\u{A}\u{A}[SS]: /url\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>\u{1E9E}</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Consecutive internal spaces, tabs, and line endings are treated as one space for\u{A}purposes of determining matching:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[Foo\u{A}  bar]: /url\u{A}\u{A}[Baz][Foo bar]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22}>Baz</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}No spaces, tabs, or line endings are allowed between the [link text] and the\u{A}[link label]:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo] [bar]\u{A}\u{A}[bar]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo] <a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>bar</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]\u{A}[bar]\u{A}\u{A}[bar]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo]\u{A}<a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>bar</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}This is a departure from John Gruber's original Markdown syntax\u{A}description, which explicitly allows whitespace between the link\u{A}text and the link label.  It brings reference links in line with\u{A}[inline links], which (according to both original Markdown and\u{A}this spec) cannot have whitespace after the link text.  More\u{A}importantly, it prevents inadvertent capture of consecutive\u{A}[shortcut reference links]. If whitespace is all");
      out.append("owed between the\u{A}link text and the link label, then in the following we will have\u{A}a single reference link, not two shortcut reference links, as\u{A}intended:\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}[foo]\u{A}[bar]\u{A}\u{A}[foo]: /url1\u{A}[bar]: /url2\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}(Note that [shortcut reference links] were introduced by Gruber\u{A}himself in a beta version of `Markdown.pl`, but never included\u{A}in the official syntax description.  Without shortcut reference\u{A}links, it is harmless to allow space between the link text and\u{A}link label; but once shortcut references are introduced, it is\u{A}too dangerous to allow this, as it frequently leads to\u{A}unintended results.)\u{A}\u{A}When there are multiple matching [link reference definitions],\u{A}the first is used:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo]: /url1\u{A}\u{A}[foo]: /url2\u{A}\u{A}[bar][foo]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url1\u{22}>bar</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that matching is performed on normalized strings, not parsed\u{A}inline content.  So the following does not match, even though the\u{A}labels define equivalent inline content:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[bar][foo\u{5C}!]\u{A}\u{A}[foo!]: /url\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[bar][foo!]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}[Link labels] cannot contain brackets, unless they are\u{A}backslash-escaped:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo][ref[]\u{A}\u{A}[ref[]: /uri\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo][ref[]</p>\u{A}<p>[ref[]: /uri</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo][ref[bar]]\u{A}\u{A}[ref[bar]]: /uri\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[foo][ref[bar]]</p>\u{A}<p>[ref[bar]]: /uri</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[[[foo]]]\u{A}\u{A}[[[foo]]]: /url\u{A}.\u{A}<p>[[[foo]]]</p>\u{A}<p>[[[foo]]]: /url</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo][ref\u{5C}[]\u{A}\u{A}[ref\u{5C}[]: /uri\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/uri\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that in this example `]` is not backslash-escaped:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[bar\u{5C}\u{5C}]: /uri\u{A}\u{A}[bar\u{5C}\u{5C}]\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/uri\u{22}>bar\u{5C}</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A [link label] mus");

The link's URI and title are\u{A}provided by the matching reference link definition.  Thus,\u{A}`[foo][]` is equivalent to `[foo][foo]`.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo][]\u{A}\u{A}[foo]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[*foo* bar][]\u{A}\u{A}[*foo* bar]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}><em>foo</em> bar</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}The link labels are case-insensitive:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[Foo][]\u{A}\u{A}[foo]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>Foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}As with full reference links, spaces, tabs, or line endings are not\u{A}allowed between the two sets of brackets:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}[foo] \u{A}[]\u{A}\u{A}[foo]: /url \u{22}title\u{22}\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}/url\u{22} title=\u{22}title\u{22}>foo</a>\u{A}[]</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A [shortcut reference link](@)\u{A}consists of a [link label] that [matches] a\u{A}[link reference definition] elsewhere in the\u{A}document and is not followed by `[]` or a link label.\u{A}The contents of the link label are parsed as inlines,\u{A}which are used as the link's text. The rules for this are the\u{A}same as for [link text], except that (a) an\u{A}image description starts with `![` rather than `[`, and\u{A}(b) an image description may contain links.\u{A}An image description has inline elements\u{A}as its contents. They are parsed as links, with the URL or email address\u{A}as the link label.\u{A}\u{A}A [URI autolink](@) consists of `<`, followed by an\u{A}[absolute URI] followed by `>`.  It is parsed as\u{A}a link to the URI, with the URI as the link's label.\u{A}\u{A}An [absolute URI](@),\u{A}for these purposes, consists of a [scheme] followed by a colon (`:`)\u{A}followed by zero or more characters other than [ASCII control\u{A}charact");
      out.append("ers][ASCII control character], [space], `<`, and `>`.\u{A}If the URI includes these characters, they must be percent-encoded\u{A}(e.g. `%20` for a space).\u{A}\u{A}For purposes of this spec, a [scheme](@) is any sequence\u{A}of 2--32 characters beginning with an ASCII letter and followed\u{A}by any combination of ASCII letters, digits, or the symbols plus\u{A}(\u{22}+\u{22}), period (\u{22}.\u{22}), or hyphen (\u{22}-\u{22}).\u{A}\u{A}Here are some valid autolinks:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<http://foo.bar.baz>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a example\u{A}<https://foo.bar.baz/test?q=hello&id=22&boolean>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}https://foo.bar.baz/test?q=hello&amp;id=22&amp;boolean\u{22}>https://foo.bar.baz/test?q=hello&amp;id=22&amp;boolean</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<irc://foo.bar:2233/baz>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}irc://foo.bar:2233/baz\u{22}>irc://foo.bar:2233/baz</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Uppercase is also fine:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<MAILTO:FOO@BAR.BAZ>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}MAILTO:FOO@BAR.BAZ\u{22}>MAILTO:FOO@BAR.BAZ</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Note that many strings that count as [absolute URIs] for\u{A}purposes of this spec are not valid URIs, because their\u{A}schemes are not registered or because of other problems\u{A}with their syntax:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a+b+c:d>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}a+b+c:d\u{22}>a+b+c:d</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<made-up-scheme://foo,bar>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}made-up-scheme://foo,bar\u{22}>made-up-scheme://foo,bar</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<https://../>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}https://../\u{22}>https://../</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<localhost:5001/foo>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}localhost:5001/foo\u{22}>localhost:5001/foo</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Spaces are not allowed in autolinks:\u{A}\u{A}```");
      out.append("````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<https://foo.bar/baz bim>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;https://foo.bar/baz bim&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Backslash-escapes do not work inside autolinks:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<https://example.com/\u{5C}[\u{5C}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}https://example.com/%5C%5B%5C\u{22}>https://example.com/\u{5C}[\u{5C}</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}An [email autolink](@)\u{A}consists of `<`, followed by an [email address],\u{A}followed by `>`.  The link's label is the email address,\u{A}and the URL is `mailto:` followed by the email address.\u{A}\u{A}An [email address](@),\u{A}for these purposes, is anything that matches\u{A}the [non-normative regex from the HTML5\u{A}spec](https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/forms.html#e-mail-state-(type=email)):\u{A}\u{A}    /^[a-zA-Z0-9.!#\u{24}%&'*+/=?^_`{|}~-]+@[a-zA-Z0-9](?:[a-zA-Z0-9-]{0,61}[a-zA-Z0-9])?\u{A}    (?:\u{5C}.[a-zA-Z0-9](?:[a-zA-Z0-9-]{0,61}[a-zA-Z0-9])?)*\u{24}/\u{A}\u{A}Examples of email autolinks:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<foo@bar.example.com>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}mailto:foo@bar.example.com\u{22}>foo@bar.example.com</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<foo+special@Bar.baz-bar0.com>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}mailto:foo+special@Bar.baz-bar0.com\u{22}>foo+special@Bar.baz-bar0.com</a></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Backslash-escapes do not work inside email autolinks:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<foo\u{5C}+@bar.example.com>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;foo+@bar.example.com&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}These are not autolinks:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}< https://foo.bar >\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt; https://foo.bar &gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<m:abc>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;m:abc&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<foo.bar.baz>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;foo.bar.baz&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}h");
      out.append("ttps://example.com\u{A}.\u{A}<p>https://example.com</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo@bar.example.com\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo@bar.example.com</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## Raw HTML\u{A}\u{A}Text between `<` and `>` that looks like an HTML tag is parsed as a\u{A}raw HTML tag and will be rendered in HTML without escaping.\u{A}Tag and attribute names are not limited to current HTML tags,\u{A}so custom tags (and even, say, DocBook tags) may be used.\u{A}\u{A}Here is the grammar for tags:\u{A}\u{A}A [tag name](@) consists of an ASCII letter\u{A}followed by zero or more ASCII letters, digits, or\u{A}hyphens (`-`).\u{A}\u{A}An [attribute](@) consists of spaces, tabs, and up to one line ending,\u{A}an [attribute name], and an optional\u{A}[attribute value specification].\u{A}\u{A}An [attribute name](@)\u{A}consists of an ASCII letter, `_`, or `:`, followed by zero or more ASCII\u{A}letters, digits, `_`, `.`, `:`, or `-`.  (Note:  This is the XML\u{A}specification restricted to ASCII.  HTML5 is laxer.)\u{A}\u{A}An [attribute value specification](@)\u{A}consists of optional spaces, tabs, and up to one line ending,\u{A}a `=` character, optional spaces, tabs, and up to one line ending,\u{A}and an [attribute value].\u{A}\u{A}An [attribute value](@)\u{A}consists of an [unquoted attribute value],\u{A}a [single-quoted attribute value], or a [double-quoted attribute value].\u{A}\u{A}An [unquoted attribute value](@)\u{A}is a nonempty string of characters not\u{A}including spaces, tabs, line endings, `\u{22}`, `'`, `=`, `<`, `>`, or `` ` ``.\u{A}\u{A}A [single-quoted attribute value](@)\u{A}consists of `'`, zero or more\u{A}characters not including `'`, and a final `'`.\u{A}\u{A}A [double-quoted attribute value](@)\u{A}consists of `\u{22}`, zero or more\u{A}characters not including `\u{22}`, and a final `\u{22}`.\u{A}\u{A}An [open tag](@) consists of a `<` character, a [tag name],\u{A}zero or more [attributes], optional spaces, tabs, and up to one line ending,\u{A}an optional `/` character, and a `>` character.\u{A}\u{A}A [closing tag](@) consists of the string `</`, a\u{A}[tag name], optional spaces, tabs, and up to one line ending, and the character\u{A}`>");
      out.append("`.\u{A}\u{A}An [HTML comment](@) consists of `<!-->`, `<!--->`, or  `<!--`, a string of\u{A}characters not including the string `-->`, and `-->` (see the\u{A}[HTML spec](https://html.spec.whatwg.org/multipage/parsing.html#markup-declaration-open-state)).\u{A}\u{A}A [processing instruction](@)\u{A}consists of the string `<?`, a string\u{A}of characters not including the string `?>`, and the string\u{A}`?>`.\u{A}\u{A}A [declaration](@) consists of the string `<!`, an ASCII letter, zero or more\u{A}characters not including the character `>`, and the character `>`.\u{A}\u{A}A [CDATA section](@) consists of\u{A}the string `<![CDATA[`, a string of characters not including the string\u{A}`]]>`, and the string `]]>`.\u{A}\u{A}An [HTML tag](@) consists of an [open tag], a [closing tag],\u{A}an [HTML comment], a [processing instruction], a [declaration],\u{A}or a [CDATA section].\u{A}\u{A}Here are some simple open tags:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a><bab><c2c>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a><bab><c2c></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Empty elements:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a/><b2/>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a/><b2/></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Whitespace is allowed:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a  /><b2\u{A}data=\u{22}foo\u{22} >\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a  /><b2\u{A}data=\u{22}foo\u{22} ></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}With attributes:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a foo=\u{22}bar\u{22} bam = 'baz <em>\u{22}</em>'\u{A}_boolean zoop:33=zoop:33 />\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a foo=\u{22}bar\u{22} bam = 'baz <em>\u{22}</em>'\u{A}_boolean zoop:33=zoop:33 /></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Custom tag names can be used:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}Foo <responsive-image src=\u{22}foo.jpg\u{22} />\u{A}.\u{A}<p>Foo <responsive-image src=\u{22}foo.jpg\u{22} /></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Illegal tag names, not parsed as HTML:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<33> <__>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;33&gt; &lt;__&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Illegal attribute names:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a h*#ref=\u{22}hi\u{22}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;a h*#ref=&quot;hi&quot;&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Illegal attribute values:\u{A}\u{A}`````");
      out.append("``````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a href=\u{22}hi'> <a href=hi'>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;a href=&quot;hi'&gt; &lt;a href=hi'&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Illegal whitespace:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}< a><\u{A}foo><bar/ >\u{A}<foo bar=baz\u{A}bim!bop />\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt; a&gt;&lt;\u{A}foo&gt;&lt;bar/ &gt;\u{A}&lt;foo bar=baz\u{A}bim!bop /&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Missing whitespace:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a href='bar'title=title>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;a href='bar'title=title&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Closing tags:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}</a></foo >\u{A}.\u{A}<p></a></foo ></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Illegal attributes in closing tag:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}</a href=\u{22}foo\u{22}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;/a href=&quot;foo&quot;&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Comments:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo <!-- this is a --\u{A}comment - with hyphens -->\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo <!-- this is a --\u{A}comment - with hyphens --></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo <!--> foo -->\u{A}\u{A}foo <!---> foo -->\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo <!--> foo --&gt;</p>\u{A}<p>foo <!---> foo --&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Processing instructions:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo <?php echo \u{24}a; ?>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo <?php echo \u{24}a; ?></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Declarations:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo <!ELEMENT br EMPTY>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo <!ELEMENT br EMPTY></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}CDATA sections:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo <![CDATA[>&<]]>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo <![CDATA[>&<]]></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Entity and numeric character references are preserved in HTML\u{A}attributes:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo <a href=\u{22}&ouml;\u{22}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo <a href=\u{22}&ouml;\u{22}></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Backslash escapes do not work in HTML attributes:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo <a href=\u{22}\u{5C}*\u{22}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo <a href=\u{22}\u{5C}*\u{22}></p>\u{A}``````````````````````````");
      out.append("``````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a href=\u{22}\u{5C}\u{22}\u{22}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p>&lt;a href=&quot;&quot;&quot;&gt;</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## Hard line breaks\u{A}\u{A}A line ending (not in a code span or HTML tag) that is preceded\u{A}by two or more spaces and does not occur at the end of a block\u{A}is parsed as a [hard line break](@) (rendered\u{A}in HTML as a `<br />` tag):\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo  \u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo<br />\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}For a more visible alternative, a backslash before the\u{A}[line ending] may be used instead of two or more spaces:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo\u{5C}\u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo<br />\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}More than two spaces can be used:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo       \u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo<br />\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Leading spaces at the beginning of the next line are ignored:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo  \u{A}     bar\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo<br />\u{A}bar</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo\u{5C}\u{A}     bar\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo<br />\u{A}bar</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Hard line breaks can occur inside emphasis, links, and other constructs\u{A}that allow inline content:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}*foo  \u{A}bar*\u{A}.\u{A}<p><em>foo<br />\u{A}bar</em></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}*foo\u{5C}\u{A}bar*\u{A}.\u{A}<p><em>foo<br />\u{A}bar</em></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Hard line breaks do not occur inside code spans\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`code  \u{A}span`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>code   span</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}`code\u{5C}\u{A}span`\u{A}.\u{A}<p><code>code\u{5C} span</code></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}or HTML tags:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a href=\u{22}foo  \u{A}bar\u{22}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}foo  \u{A}bar\u{22}></p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}<a href=\u{22}foo\u{5C}\u{A}bar\u{22}>\u{A}.\u{A}<p><a href=\u{22}foo\u{5C}\u{A}bar\u{22}></p>\u{A}");
      out.append("````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Hard line breaks are for separating inline content within a block.\u{A}Neither syntax for hard line breaks works at the end of a paragraph or\u{A}other block element:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo\u{5C}\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo\u{5C}</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo  \u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}### foo\u{5C}\u{A}.\u{A}<h3>foo\u{5C}</h3>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}### foo  \u{A}.\u{A}<h3>foo</h3>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}## Soft line breaks\u{A}\u{A}A regular line ending (not in a code span or HTML tag) that is not\u{A}preceded by two or more spaces or a backslash is parsed as a\u{A}[softbreak](@).  (A soft line break may be rendered in HTML either as a\u{A}[line ending] or as a space. The result will be the same in\u{A}browsers. In the examples here, a [line ending] will be used.)\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo\u{A}baz\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Spaces at the end of the line and beginning of the next line are\u{A}removed:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}foo \u{A} baz\u{A}.\u{A}<p>foo\u{A}baz</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}A conforming parser may render a soft line break in HTML either as a\u{A}line ending or as a space.\u{A}\u{A}A renderer may also provide an option to render soft line breaks\u{A}as hard line breaks.\u{A}\u{A}## Textual content\u{A}\u{A}Any characters not given an interpretation by the above rules will\u{A}be parsed as plain textual content.\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}hello \u{24}.;'there\u{A}.\u{A}<p>hello \u{24}.;'there</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}Foo \u{3C7}\u{3C1}\u{1FC6}\u{3BD}\u{A}.\u{A}<p>Foo \u{3C7}\u{3C1}\u{1FC6}\u{3BD}</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}Internal spaces are preserved verbatim:\u{A}\u{A}```````````````````````````````` example\u{A}Multiple     spaces\u{A}.\u{A}<p>Multiple     spaces</p>\u{A}````````````````````````````````\u{A}\u{A}\u{A}<!-- END TESTS -->\u{A}\u{A}# Appendix: A parsing strategy\u{A}\u{A}In this appendix we describe some features ");
      out.append("of the parsing strategy\u{A}used in the CommonMark reference implementations.\u{A}\u{A}## Overview\u{A}\u{A}Parsing has two phases:\u{A}\u{A}1. In the first phase, lines of input are consumed and the block\u{A}structure of the document---its division into paragraphs, block quotes,\u{A}list items, and so on---is constructed.  Text is assigned to these\u{A}blocks but not parsed. Link reference definitions are parsed and a\u{A}map of links is constructed.\u{A}\u{A}2. In the second phase, the raw text contents of paragraphs and headings\u{A}are parsed into sequences of Markdown inline elements (strings,\u{A}code spans, links, emphasis, and so on), using the map of link\u{A}references constructed in phase 1.\u{A}\u{A}At each point in processing, the document is represented as a tree of\u{A}**blocks**.  The root of the tree is a `document` block.  The `document`\u{A}may have any number of other blocks as **children**.  These children\u{A}may, in turn, have other blocks as children.  The last child of a block\u{A}is normally considered **open**, meaning that subsequent lines of input\u{A}can alter its contents.  (Blocks that are not open are **closed**.)\u{A}Here, for example, is a possible document tree, with the open blocks\u{A}marked by arrows:\u{A}\u{A}``` tree\u{A}-> document\u{A}  -> block_quote\u{A}       paragraph\u{A}         \u{22}Lorem ipsum dolor\u{5C}nsit amet.\u{22}\u{A}    -> list (type=bullet tight=true bullet_char=-)\u{A}         list_item\u{A}           paragraph\u{A}             \u{22}Qui *quodsi iracundia*\u{22}\u{A}      -> list_item\u{A}        -> paragraph\u{A}             \u{22}aliquando id\u{22}\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}## Phase 1: block structure\u{A}\u{A}Each line that is processed has an effect on this tree.  The line is\u{A}analyzed and, depending on its contents, the document may be altered\u{A}in one or more of the following ways:\u{A}\u{A}1. One or more open blocks may be closed.\u{A}2. One or more new blocks may be created as children of the\u{A}   last open block.\u{A}3. Text may be added to the last (deepest) open block remaining\u{A}   on the tree.\u{A}\u{A}Once a line has been incorporated into the tree in this way,\u{A}it can be discarded, so input can be read in a stream.\u{A}\u{A}For each line, ");
      out.append("we follow this procedure:\u{A}\u{A}1. First we iterate through the open blocks, starting with the\u{A}root document, and descending through last children down to the last\u{A}open block.  Each block imposes a condition that the line must satisfy\u{A}if the block is to remain open.  For example, a block quote requires a\u{A}`>` character.  A paragraph requires a non-blank line.\u{A}In this phase we may match all or just some of the open\u{A}blocks.  But we cannot close unmatched blocks yet, because we may have a\u{A}[lazy continuation line].\u{A}\u{A}2.  Next, after consuming the continuation markers for existing\u{A}blocks, we look for new block starts (e.g. `>` for a block quote).\u{A}If we encounter a new block start, we close any blocks unmatched\u{A}in step 1 before creating the new block as a child of the last\u{A}matched container block.\u{A}\u{A}3.  Finally, we look at the remainder of the line (after block\u{A}markers like `>`, list markers, and indentation have been consumed).\u{A}This is text that can be incorporated into the last open\u{A}block (a paragraph, code block, heading, or raw HTML).\u{A}\u{A}Setext headings are formed when we see a line of a paragraph\u{A}that is a [setext heading underline].\u{A}\u{A}Reference link definitions are detected when a paragraph is closed;\u{A}the accumulated text lines are parsed to see if they begin with\u{A}one or more reference link definitions.  Any remainder becomes a\u{A}normal paragraph.\u{A}\u{A}We can see how this works by considering how the tree above is\u{A}generated by four lines of Markdown:\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}> Lorem ipsum dolor\u{A}sit amet.\u{A}> - Qui *quodsi iracundia*\u{A}> - aliquando id\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}At the outset, our document model is just\u{A}\u{A}``` tree\u{A}-> document\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The first line of our text,\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}> Lorem ipsum dolor\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}causes a `block_quote` block to be created as a child of our\u{A}open `document` block, and a `paragraph` block as a child of\u{A}the `block_quote`.  Then the text is added to the last open\u{A}block, the `paragraph`:\u{A}\u{A}``` tree\u{A}-> document\u{A}  -> block_quote\u{A}    -> paragraph\u{A}         \u{22}Lorem ipsum dolor\u{22}\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The next line,\u{A}\u{A}`");
      out.append("`` markdown\u{A}sit amet.\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}is a \u{22}lazy continuation\u{22} of the open `paragraph`, so it gets added\u{A}to the paragraph's text:\u{A}\u{A}``` tree\u{A}-> document\u{A}  -> block_quote\u{A}    -> paragraph\u{A}         \u{22}Lorem ipsum dolor\u{5C}nsit amet.\u{22}\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The third line,\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}> - Qui *quodsi iracundia*\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}causes the `paragraph` block to be closed, and a new `list` block\u{A}opened as a child of the `block_quote`.  A `list_item` is also\u{A}added as a child of the `list`, and a `paragraph` as a child of\u{A}the `list_item`.  The text is then added to the new `paragraph`:\u{A}\u{A}``` tree\u{A}-> document\u{A}  -> block_quote\u{A}       paragraph\u{A}         \u{22}Lorem ipsum dolor\u{5C}nsit amet.\u{22}\u{A}    -> list (type=bullet tight=true bullet_char=-)\u{A}      -> list_item\u{A}        -> paragraph\u{A}             \u{22}Qui *quodsi iracundia*\u{22}\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The fourth line,\u{A}\u{A}``` markdown\u{A}> - aliquando id\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}causes the `list_item` (and its child the `paragraph`) to be closed,\u{A}and a new `list_item` opened up as child of the `list`.  A `paragraph`\u{A}is added as a child of the new `list_item`, to contain the text.\u{A}We thus obtain the final tree:\u{A}\u{A}``` tree\u{A}-> document\u{A}  -> block_quote\u{A}       paragraph\u{A}         \u{22}Lorem ipsum dolor\u{5C}nsit amet.\u{22}\u{A}    -> list (type=bullet tight=true bullet_char=-)\u{A}         list_item\u{A}           paragraph\u{A}             \u{22}Qui *quodsi iracundia*\u{22}\u{A}      -> list_item\u{A}        -> paragraph\u{A}             \u{22}aliquando id\u{22}\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}## Phase 2: inline structure\u{A}\u{A}Once all of the input has been parsed, all open blocks are closed.\u{A}\u{A}We then \u{22}walk the tree,\u{22} visiting every node, and parse raw\u{A}string contents of paragraphs and headings as inlines.  At this\u{A}point we have seen all the link reference definitions, so we can\u{A}resolve reference links as we go.\u{A}\u{A}``` tree\u{A}document\u{A}  block_quote\u{A}    paragraph\u{A}      str \u{22}Lorem ipsum dolor\u{22}\u{A}      softbreak\u{A}      str \u{22}sit amet.\u{22}\u{A}    list (type=bullet tight=true bullet_char=-)\u{A}      list_item\u{A}        paragraph\u{A}          str \u{22}Qui \u{22}\u{A}          emph\u{A}            str \u{22}quodsi iracundia\u{22}\u{A}      list_item\u{A}        paragraph\u{A}          str \u{22}aliqu");
      out.append("ando id\u{22}\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}Notice how the [line ending] in the first paragraph has\u{A}been parsed as a `softbreak`, and the asterisks in the first list item\u{A}have become an `emph`.\u{A}\u{A}### An algorithm for parsing nested emphasis and links\u{A}\u{A}By far the trickiest part of inline parsing is handling emphasis,\u{A}strong emphasis, links, and images.  This is done using the following\u{A}algorithm.\u{A}\u{A}When we're parsing inlines and we hit either\u{A}\u{A}- a run of `*` or `_` characters, or\u{A}- a `[` or `![`\u{A}\u{A}we insert a text node with these symbols as its literal content, and we\u{A}add a pointer to this text node to the [delimiter stack](@).\u{A}\u{A}The [delimiter stack] is a doubly linked list.  Each\u{A}element contains a pointer to a text node, plus information about\u{A}\u{A}- the type of delimiter (`[`, `![`, `*`, `_`)\u{A}- the number of delimiters,\u{A}- whether the delimiter is \u{22}active\u{22} (all are active to start), and\u{A}- whether the delimiter is a potential opener, a potential closer,\u{A}  or both (which depends on what sort of characters precede\u{A}  and follow the delimiters).\u{A}\u{A}When we hit a `]` character, we call the *look for link or image*\u{A}procedure (see below).\u{A}\u{A}When we hit the end of the input, we call the *process emphasis*\u{A}procedure (see below), with `stack_bottom` = NULL.\u{A}\u{A}#### *look for link or image*\u{A}\u{A}Starting at the top of the delimiter stack, we look backwards\u{A}through the stack for an opening `[` or `![` delimiter.\u{A}\u{A}- If we don't find one, we return a literal text node `]`.\u{A}\u{A}- If we do find one, but it's not *active*, we remove the inactive\u{A}  delimiter from the stack, and return a literal text node `]`.\u{A}\u{A}- If we find one and it's active, then we parse ahead to see if\u{A}  we have an inline link/image, reference link/image, collapsed reference\u{A}  link/image, or shortcut reference link/image.\u{A}\u{A}  + If we don't, then we remove the opening delimiter from the\u{A}    delimiter stack and return a literal text node `]`.\u{A}\u{A}  + If we do, then\u{A}\u{A}    * We return a link or image node whose children are the inlines\u{A}      after the text node pointed ");
      out.append("to by the opening delimiter.\u{A}\u{A}    * We run *process emphasis* on these inlines, with the `[` opener\u{A}      as `stack_bottom`.\u{A}\u{A}    * We remove the opening delimiter.\u{A}\u{A}    * If we have a link (and not an image), we also set all\u{A}      `[` delimiters before the opening delimiter to *inactive*.  (This\u{A}      will prevent us from getting links within links.)\u{A}\u{A}#### *process emphasis*\u{A}\u{A}Parameter `stack_bottom` sets a lower bound to how far we\u{A}descend in the [delimiter stack].  If it is NULL, we can\u{A}go all the way to the bottom.  Otherwise, we stop before\u{A}visiting `stack_bottom`.\u{A}\u{A}Let `current_position` point to the element on the [delimiter stack]\u{A}just above `stack_bottom` (or the first element if `stack_bottom`\u{A}is NULL).\u{A}\u{A}We keep track of the `openers_bottom` for each delimiter\u{A}type (`*`, `_`), indexed to the length of the closing delimiter run\u{A}(modulo 3) and to whether the closing delimiter can also be an\u{A}opener.  Initialize this to `stack_bottom`.\u{A}\u{A}Then we repeat the following until we run out of potential\u{A}closers:\u{A}\u{A}- Move `current_position` forward in the delimiter stack (if needed)\u{A}  until we find the first potential closer with delimiter `*` or `_`.\u{A}  (This will be the potential closer closest\u{A}  to the beginning of the input -- the first one in parse order.)\u{A}\u{A}- Now, look back in the stack (staying above `stack_bottom` and\u{A}  the `openers_bottom` for this delimiter type) for the\u{A}  first matching potential opener (\u{22}matching\u{22} means same delimiter).\u{A}\u{A}- If one is found:\u{A}\u{A}  + Figure out whether we have emphasis or strong emphasis:\u{A}    if both closer and opener spans have length >= 2, we have\u{A}    strong, otherwise regular.\u{A}\u{A}  + Insert an emph or strong emph node accordingly, after\u{A}    the text node corresponding to the opener.\u{A}\u{A}  + Remove any delimiters between the opener and closer from\u{A}    the delimiter stack.\u{A}\u{A}  + Remove 1 (for regular emph) or 2 (for strong emph) delimiters\u{A}    from the opening and closing text nodes.  If they become empty\u{A}    as a result, remove them and ");
      out.append("remove the corresponding element\u{A}    of the delimiter stack.  If the closing node is removed, reset\u{A}    `current_position` to the next element in the stack.\u{A}\u{A}- If none is found:\u{A}\u{A}  + Set `openers_bottom` to the element before `current_position`.\u{A}    (We know that there are no openers for this kind of closer up to and\u{A}    including this point, so this puts a lower bound on future searches.)\u{A}\u{A}  + If the closer at `current_position` is not a potential opener,\u{A}    remove it from the delimiter stack (since we know it can't\u{A}    be a closer either).\u{A}\u{A}  + Advance `current_position` to the next element in the stack.\u{A}\u{A}After we're done, we remove all delimiters above `stack_bottom` from the\u{A}delimiter stack.\u{A}");
      out.toString()
    }

    let chromariteReadme(): String {
      let out = new StringBuilder();
      out.append("# Chromarite\u{A}\u{A}A cross-language color transformation library, working toward the color math of\u{A}[CSS Color 4][CssColor4] and the mixing of [CSS Color 5][CssColor5].\u{A}\u{A}## Examples\u{A}\u{A}In Temper, and the same API in each [supported language](#languages):\u{A}\u{A}```temper\u{A}// Most channels go from 0 to 1, and hue is in degrees, as in CSS.\u{A}let red = Color.fromList(Space.srgb, [1.0, 0.0, 0.0]);\u{A}let lch = red.toSpace(Space.oklch).toList(); // About [0.628, 0.258, 29.23]\u{A}\u{A}// Parse any CSS color, and format as hex or CSS.\u{A}let purple = Color.parse(\u{22}rebeccapurple\u{22});\u{A}console.log(purple.toHex()[0]); // #663399\u{A}console.log(purple.toCss()[0]); // rgb(102 51 153)\u{A}let teal = Color.parse(\u{22}oklch(65% 0.1 200 / 80%)\u{22});\u{A}console.log(teal.toHex()[0]); // #2ba1a7cc\u{A}\u{A}// Many colors at once, one per row, with alpha after each row's channels.\u{A}let vivid = Color.fromListWithAlpha(Space.oklch, [\u{A}  0.7, 0.1, 0.1, 1.0,\u{A}  0.6, 0.4, 0.75, 0.8,\u{A}]);\u{A}// Fit them into sRGB as CSS does, keeping lightness and hue.\u{A}console.log(vivid.toGamut(Space.srgb).toHex()[1]); // #4d69ffcc\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}## Status\u{A}\u{A}Working now:\u{A}\u{A}- Conversion between sRGB, linear sRGB, HSL, HSV, HWB, Oklab, OkLCh, Display\u{A}  P3, A98 RGB, ProPhoto RGB, Rec. 2020, XYZ (D65 and D50), CIE Lab, and CIE\u{A}  LCH, for single colors or in bulk\u{A}- Optional alpha, given and read inline with the channels in any space, and\u{A}  stored apart so conversions skip it\u{A}- Parsing CSS Color 4 syntax: hex, named colors, and `rgb()`, `hsl()`,\u{A}  `hwb()`, `lab()`, `lch()`, `oklab()`, `oklch()` and `color()`, in modern\u{A}  or legacy comma syntax\u{A}- Formatting as CSS, `#rrggbb` or `#rrggbbaa` strings, and packed ints\u{A}- Out-of-range values kept exact through conversions\u{A}- Gamut mapping as in CSS Color 4, reducing chroma in OkLCh to fit sRGB,\u{A}  Display P3 or another RGB gamut, used for hex output, plus clamping on\u{A}  request and the deltaEOK color difference\u{A}- Mixing and gradients as in CSS Color 4 and 5, in Oklab by default or any\u{A}  space, with CSS's hue paths and premultiplied alpha\u{A}- Lightening");
      out.append(", saturating, hue rotation and grayscale in OkLCh, so changes\u{A}  look even across colors\u{A}- WCAG luminance and contrast ratios, and fitting colors to reach a ratio\u{A}\u{A}Also: a [tour](demos/README.md) of the whole API in each language, with\u{A}identical output, and a [site](site/) to try colors in the browser.\u{A}\u{A}Planned:\u{A}\u{A}- Running more languages directly on the demo site\u{A}- Later: NumPy arrays for Python, CSS relative color syntax, and more color\u{A}  difference formulas\u{A}\u{A}Chromarite uses material from CSS Color 4 and web-platform-tests. See\u{A}[NOTICE](NOTICE).\u{A}\u{A}## Languages\u{A}\u{A}Being made in Temper, Chromarite supports these languages automatically, and its\u{A}tests run on each:\u{A}\u{A}- C++\u{A}- C\u{5C}#\u{A}- Java\u{A}- JS\u{A}- Lua\u{A}- Python\u{A}- Rust\u{A}\u{A}This library is also a testing ground for how well we can support specific\u{A}language needs. For example, it would be nice to support NumPy arrays of colors\u{A}out of the box for Python. This library might help figure out what Temper still\u{A}needs for things like that.\u{A}\u{A}## Development\u{A}\u{A}Developed primarily with AI coding (specifically Claude Code on Opus 5.5) as of\u{A}October 2026.\u{A}\u{A}Run tests with [Temper][Temper], on one backend while working:\u{A}\u{A}    temper test -b py\u{A}\u{A}Or on all of them:\u{A}\u{A}    temper test -b cpp -b csharp -b java -b js -b lua -b py -b rust\u{A}\u{A}Run the API tour in every language against `temper.out/`, checking each\u{A}prints the same text (see [demos](demos/README.md)):\u{A}\u{A}    uv run demos/run.py\u{A}\u{A}Build and preview the site, which uses the JS backend's output, and the\u{A}Rust backend's compiled to wasm:\u{A}\u{A}    temper build -b js -b rust\u{A}    rustup target add wasm32-unknown-unknown\u{A}    npm ci --prefix site\u{A}    uv run site/build.py\u{A}    python3 -m http.server --bind 127.0.0.1 -d site/build\u{A}\u{A}CI runs the tests and the tour on every backend for pull requests and pushes\u{A}to `main`, using Temper's nightly `dev` build, and publishes the site from\u{A}`main`. Source files in `src/` are literate Markdown, with tests next to the\u{A}code they cover.\u{A}\u{A}[CssColor4]: https://www.w3.org/TR/css-color-");
      out.append("4/\u{A}[CssColor5]: https://www.w3.org/TR/css-color-5/\u{A}[Temper]: https://temperlang.dev/\u{A}\u{A}## License\u{A}\u{A}Apache-2.0 OR MIT, at your option, as Temper is; see [COPYRIGHT](COPYRIGHT). Third-party material, with its notices, is in [NOTICE](NOTICE).\u{A}");
      out.toString()
    }

    let hrefineReadme(): String {
      let out = new StringBuilder();
      out.append("# Hrefine\u{A}\u{A}Parses and serializes URLs exactly as the [WHATWG URL\u{A}Standard](https://url.spec.whatwg.org/) says, which browsers follow,\u{A}with the same results in every language.\u{A}\u{A}Most languages' own URL parsers follow older rules, or their own, so\u{A}the same URL can name a different host in Python, Java, C#, and the\u{A}browser. Those gaps have caused real security holes: server-side\u{A}request forgery and allowlist bypasses, where a check reads a URL one\u{A}way and the request goes another. This library reads every URL as the\u{A}standard says, in every language it supports, checked against the\u{A}browsers' own test cases.\u{A}\u{A}Parsing and serializing work on every backend, international domain\u{A}names included, and so do the standard's setters and search parameters\u{A}(`URLSearchParams`), a strict mode, and policies: the usual security\u{A}checks (schemes, credentials, private and loopback addresses, host\u{A}allowlists, ports, and redirect targets) in one call, on the parsed URL, with\u{A}a preset for URLs a server fetches. Credentials (the username and\u{A}password written in the URL, as in `https://user:pass@host/`; not\u{A}cookies, headers, or tokens in the query) are hard to expose: `href`\u{A}leaves them out, `toString` masks them, the display form leaves them\u{A}out, and policies never echo them. And for people and tools: a display form, as browsers\u{A}show URLs, comparisons, and where each part was written. See the [plan](docs/plan.md) for\u{A}what's next.\u{A}\u{A}## Examples\u{A}\u{A}In Temper, and the same API in each supported language (to refine\u{A}before 1.0). The names are JavaScript's, so `parseUrl` throws (bubbles)\u{A}as `new URL()` does, and `Url.parse` gives null:\u{A}\u{A}```temper\u{A}let url = parseUrl(\u{22}https://user@EXAMPLE.com:443/a/../b?q=1#top\u{22}) orelse panic();\u{A}url.href;     // \u{22}https://example.com/b?q=1#top\u{22}, to hand on: no credentials\u{A}url.toString(); // \u{22}https://***@example.com/b?q=1#top\u{22}, for logs\u{A}url.hrefWithCredentials(); // \u{22}https://user@example.com/b?q=1#top\u{22}, only to send them\u{A}url.hostname; // \u{22}example.com\u{22} (lowercased; the defa");
      out.append("ult port is dropped)\u{A}url.pathname; // \u{22}/b\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}// Relative to a base, given as a string, or parsed once and reused.\u{A}(parseUrl(\u{22}../c\u{22}, \u{22}https://example.com/a/b\u{22}) orelse panic()).href; // \u{22}https://example.com/c\u{22}\u{A}let base = parseUrl(\u{22}https://example.com/a/b\u{22}) orelse panic();\u{A}(base.resolve(\u{22}../c\u{22}) orelse panic()).href; // \u{22}https://example.com/c\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}// Inputs that aren't URLs bubble, or give null, or say why.\u{A}Url.parse(\u{22}https://exa mple.com\u{22});     // null\u{A}Url.canParse(\u{22}https://exa mple.com\u{22});  // false\u{A}(checkUrl(\u{22}https://exa mple.com\u{22}) orelse panic()).errors; // [domain-invalid-code-point at 8]\u{A}\u{A}// Typed parts, beside the standard's strings.\u{A}url.scheme;     // \u{22}https\u{22}\u{A}url.portNumber; // null (443 is the default)\u{A}url.hostKind;   // HostKind.domain\u{A}(parseUrl(\u{22}http://0x7f.1/\u{22}) orelse panic()).hostKind; // HostKind.ipv4, 127.0.0.1\u{A}url.sameOrigin(base); // true\u{A}\u{A}// International domain names, as browsers read them.\u{A}(parseUrl(\u{22}https://B\u{FC}cher.example\u{22}) orelse panic()).hostname; // \u{22}xn--bcher-kva.example\u{22}\u{A}domainToUnicode(\u{22}xn--bcher-kva.example\u{22}); // \u{22}b\u{FC}cher.example\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}// The strict RFC 3986 form, for parsers such as Java's URI.\u{A}(parseUrl(\u{22}https://example.com/a|b?q={x}\u{22}) orelse panic()).strictHref; // \u{22}https://example.com/a%7Cb?q=%7Bx%7D\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}// Setters give a new URL; url itself never changes.\u{A}let moved = url.withHost(\u{22}example.org:8443\u{22}).withPathname(\u{22}/c d\u{22}).withHash(\u{22}\u{22});\u{A}moved.href; // \u{22}https://example.org:8443/c%20d?q=1\u{22}\u{A}url.withPort(\u{22}x\u{22}).port; // \u{22}\u{22} (as in JavaScript, a value that can't be set is ignored)\u{A}url.withPortNumber(8443).port; // \u{22}8443\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}// Search parameters: a copy of the query, changed in place, then put back.\u{A}let params = moved.searchParams;\u{A}params.append(\u{22}lang\u{22}, \u{22}en US\u{22});\u{A}params.set(\u{22}q\u{22}, \u{22}2\u{22});\u{A}moved.withSearchParams(params).search; // \u{22}?q=2&lang=en+US\u{22}\u{A}UrlSearchParams.fromEntries([new Pair(\u{22}q\u{22}, \u{22}a b\u{22})]).toString(); // \u{22}q=a+b\u{22}\u{A}\u{A}// Strict mode: no validation errors, as cautious parsers take URLs.\u{A}(checkUrl(\u{22}http://0x7f.1/\u{22}) orelse panic()).strictUrl; // null: IPv4-non-decim");
      out.append("al-part, IPv4-non-canonical\u{A}\u{A}// Policies: built once, a rule at a time, then checked against many URLs.\u{A}// UrlPolicy.fetch is https only, strict, with no credentials, special hosts,\u{A}// or ports but the default. A bad allowlist entry bubbles.\u{A}let policy = UrlPolicy.fetch.withSubdomainsOf([\u{22}example.com\u{22}]) orelse panic();\u{A}policy.check(\u{22}https://api.example.com/\u{22}).url;  // the URL\u{A}policy.check(\u{22}https://127.0.0.1/\u{22}).toString(); // \u{22}specialHost: 127.0.0.1 is loopback (RFC 1122), in 127.0.0.0/8\u{22}\u{A}url.withoutCredentials(); // a Url without the username and password\u{A}\u{A}// For people, never for decisions: Unicode hosts where browsers show them,\u{A}// and no credentials.\u{A}let shown = UrlDisplay.addressBar;\u{A}shown.format(parseUrl(\u{22}https://user@xn--bcher-kva.example/\u{22}) orelse panic()); // \u{22}https://b\u{FC}cher.example\u{22}\u{A}domainToDisplay(\u{22}xn--pple-43d.com\u{22}); // \u{22}xn--pple-43d.com\u{22}, since it's Cyrillic \u{430} and Latin pple\u{A}url.sameDocument(url.withHash(\u{22}other\u{22})); // true: the same but for the fragment\u{A}\u{A}// Where each part was written, for editors and linters.\u{A}let source = parseUrlSource(\u{22} https://EXAMPLE.com/a\u{5C}tb\u{22}) orelse panic();\u{A}source.hostSpan.asWritten; // \u{22}EXAMPLE.com\u{22}, at 9, 11 long; source.url is for decisions\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}The [tour](demos/README.md) shows the same in each language, with\u{A}identical output.\u{A}\u{A}## API\u{A}\u{A}- `parseUrl(input, base?)`: the standard's URL parser, as `new URL()`\u{A}  in JavaScript: a `Url`, or it bubbles (throws) where the standard\u{A}  fails. `Url.parse(input, base?)` gives null instead, and\u{A}  `Url.canParse(input, base?)` only says whether it would parse.\u{A}- `checkUrl(input, base?)`: a `UrlCheck`, with the `url` (or null) and\u{A}  the standard's validation errors, each with its `name` (such as\u{A}  `invalid-URL-unit` or `host-invalid-code-point`) and `position`, in\u{A}  code points. Where parsing fails, the last error says why, but a\u{A}  `base` that isn't a URL bubbles, as in `parseUrl`. Most\u{A}  errors don't stop a URL from parsing, as browsers accept it. Parsing\u{A}  notes errors only when asked, so `pa");

      out.append("als()`, only to send credentials deliberately;\u{A}- `toString()`, for logs.\u{A}\u{A}## Using it safely\u{A}\u{A}Hrefine doesn't change how other code reads URLs: `fetch`, `requests`,\u{A}`HttpClient`, and the rest keep their own parsers. So decide with\u{A}Hrefine, then hand on its `href`, never the original string:\u{A}\u{A}```temper\u{A}let url = Url.parse(input);\u{A}if (\u{A}  url != null && url.scheme == \u{22}https\u{22} && url.hostKind == HostKind.domain &&\u{A}    isAllowed(url.hostname) // your check\u{A}) {\u{A}  fetchUrl(url.href); // your HTTP client, given href, not input\u{A}}\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}`hostKind` says whether the host is a name or an address, however the\u{A}address is written (`0x7f.1` is `127.0.0.1`), so an allowlist of names\u{A}can't be met by an address. To redirect to a user's `next` parameter,\u{A}resolve it against your own site, and check `sameOrigin`:\u{A}\u{A}```temper\u{A}let next = site.tryResolve(param);\u{A}if (next != null && next.sameOrigin(site)) { redirectTo(next.href) }\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}Or let a policy do it all in one call, built once, with the rules\u{A}people tend to miss by hand: no credentials (`good.example@evil.example`),\u{A}no private or loopback addresses however they're written (`127.1`,\u{A}`[::ffff:127.0.0.1]`, `localhost.`), and no ports but the default\u{A}(`:6379`). `UrlPolicy.fetch` has those, with `https` only and strict\u{A}mode. Add your allowlist: `withHosts` for exact hosts, and\u{A}`withSubdomainsOf` for domains and the names under them. Each bubbles\u{A}on an entry that isn't a host, such as `example.com:443` or\u{A}`*.example.com`, rather than letting it match nothing, and each call\u{A}replaces its list, not adds to it. `withPorts` allows more ports.\u{A}\u{A}```temper\u{A}let fetchPolicy = UrlPolicy.fetch.withSubdomainsOf([\u{22}example.com\u{22}]) orelse panic();\u{A}let checked = fetchPolicy.check(input);\u{A}if (checked.url != null) { fetchUrl(checked.url.href) } else { log(checked.toString()) }\u{A}\u{A}let redirects = UrlPolicy.any.withBase(site).withPathOnly(true);\u{A}```\u{A}\u{A}A policy can't see where DNS sends a name, so against SSRF, also check\u{A}the address your client connects to, and check ");
      out.append("or turn off redirects.\u{A}\u{A}Keep credentials out of logs: `href` has none, and `url.toString()`\u{A}masks them, showing that there were some; or log a policy's `reason`.\u{A}Never log `hrefWithCredentials()` or the input. If a request needs the\u{A}credentials in the URL, send `hrefWithCredentials()` deliberately; handed\u{A}`href`, it fails to authenticate rather than leaking them. Hrefine can't tell which query parameters or fragments hold\u{A}secrets (`?token=`, `#access_token=`), so don't log URLs that may carry\u{A}tokens there; a masking helper is a possible addition (see the\u{A}[plan](docs/plan.md), \u{22}Credentials\u{22}).\u{A}\u{A}`href` is the standard's canonical form: backslashes become slashes,\u{A}tabs and newlines are gone, hosts are lowercased and in ASCII (`127.1`\u{A}becomes `127.0.0.1`, international names become Punycode), and dot\u{A}segments are resolved. Most of what makes parsers disagree is gone, so\u{A}other parsers, browsers' and servers' alike, read it as Hrefine did.\u{A}For clients that follow RFC 3986 strictly, such as Java's `URI`, hand on\u{A}`strictHref`, which escapes the few characters `href` leaves that RFC\u{A}3986 doesn't allow; on the site's recorded URLs, every parser reads it\u{A}as browsers read the original.\u{A}\u{A}## Tests\u{A}\u{A}The tests are [web-platform-tests](https://github.com/web-platform-tests/wpt)'\u{A}own URL cases, the ones browsers are checked against, vendored in\u{A}[tests/wpt](tests/wpt) at a pinned commit (see [NOTICE](NOTICE)):\u{A}\u{A}- `urltestdata.json`: 896 inputs, with bases, that must parse to given\u{A}  values for each part, or must fail. `scripts/wpt_tests.py` turns them\u{A}  into [a Temper test](src/wpt-url-tests.temper.md). All pass on every\u{A}  backend.\u{A}- `toascii.json` and `IdnaTestV2.json`: 87 and 2,675 international domain\u{A}  names, each with the host it must give, or none if it must fail.\u{A}  `scripts/wpt_idna_tests.py` turns them into [a Temper\u{A}  test](src/wpt-idna-tests.temper.md), which parses each as a URL's\u{A}  host, as web-platform-tests does. All pass on every backend.\u{A}- `urlencoded-parser.any.js` and `urlsearchparams-sort.any.js`: 35\u{A}  queries and the names and values they must give, and 8 to sort, which\u{A}  `scripts/wpt_search_params_tests.py` turns into [a Temper\u{A}  test](src/wpt-search-params-tests.temper.md) (with Node, since they're\u{A}  JavaScript). The other `urlsearchparams-*.any.js` files and\u{A}  `url-searchparams.any.js` are ported by hand into\u{A}  [search-params](src/search-params.temper.md)'s tests, but for what\u{A}  tests JavaScript's own conversions (`null` and numbers as names). All\u{A}  pass on every backend.\u{A}- `percent-encoding.json`: 7 inputs and how a query and fragment must\u{A}  escape them, ported by hand into [strict](src/strict.temper.md)'s\u{A}  tests.
